a British Tariff on imports would cause a proportionate lowering of prices by the foreign producers of semi-manufactured metals and of fully-manufactured machinery —because they could not afford to lose the British market. _

Considering the immense variety, of the metallic and engineering industries, and the wide range of difference in the profits of different manufacturers, it would be absurd for either Free Traders or Tariff Reformers to assert a universal rale as to the conse- quences in this respect of all tariffs. , In some special industries, of the nature of monopolies or semi-monopolies, the margin of profit is doubtless wide enough to permit, of some reductions of prices of the kind thus indicated by Tariff Reformers. But these " specialities " are, of course, exceptions. The great bulk of the metallic and engineer- ing industries are keenly competitive. Those which are not already so mostly exist in con- stant apprehension of competition. Most of their prices and profits, therefore, are already cut to the narrowest practical working mar- gin. As regards these, even alO per cent. to 15 per cent Tariff would have the effect of exclusion.

THE TARIFF REFORM DILEMMA.

In this direction Tariff Reformers never fairly Pace the logical dilemma created by their own contradictory forecasts. If the foreign producer can afford to lowe: prices far enough to meet the duties, then his products will come in as before, and there will be no additional employment for home workers. If the Tariff excludes his products, then these will not come; there will be no revenue from them ; and the home purchaser will have to pay the extra price equivalent to the Tariff, which leaves him less to spend on other things.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EIETAL INDUSTRIES lIND FREE TRADE.

A GENERAL VIEW.

The fundamental difference in essential principle and in practical outlook Iptween the Free Trade and. Tariff " Reform " posi- tions comes out even more clearly, perhaps, in regard to the Metal Industries than in regard to (for example) Agriculture on the one hand or Textiles on the other. The unlimited variety of the Metal Industries, ranging from the production of the raw metals to the making of the most complex machines, gives the widest possible oppor- tunity for each .individual Tariff Reformer to exhibit his greed for specially securing, by Tariff Imposts, his own section of industry against foreign competition, and to exhibit at the same time his total indifference either to the effect of his demands upon the well-being of the nation at large, or indeed, to the interests of his fellow-manufacturers who are engaged in a rather different line of production. It would be impossible, within moderate space, to examine in detail the elaborate treatises upon the metallic and the engineer- ing industries which have been issued by the Birmingham Tariff Reform Committee. For the present purpose, however, such examina- tion is altogether unnecessary, inasmuch as these Tariff Reform volumes are made up in -bulk of the constant iteration, by hundreds of manufacturing producers, of one uniform thought and demand, namely: "Such and such a foreign competitor is able, for various reasons, to produce more cheaply than I can, and to sell in this country more cheaply. Therefore I think that his products when sent to this country should be Tariff-taxed in such degree as will force him to demand, from his British customers, prices so high as will enable me to sell either at a profit or at a greater profit than now." ARTIFICIALLY-MADE EMPLOYMENT CAUSES OTHER UNEMPLOLMENT. The broad general plea urged in support of this demand for all-round protection of the metal and engineering industries is that the exclusion of the foreign manufactures (so far as the Tariff does exclude them) will result in these manufactures being produced in this country, thus affording greatly-increased. home employment to British capital and labour. The broad general reply to this, upon the fundamental principles of Free Trade, is that the Tariff Duties may truly enough stimulate employment in those sections of home industry favoured by the Tariff; but that they can only do so by raising the home prices of the protected manufactures; and the inevitable result of the higher prices is to reduce employment in some industries at least as much as the Tariff Imposts increase employment in other industries. The higher prices take proportionately larger sums out of the pockets of the purchasers of the pro- tected articles. Therefore these purchasers are so much the less able to buy other com- modities upon which their money surplus would otherwise have been expended; and the producers of the other commodities are proportionately deprived of employment. For example, let us assume that at present a British farmer is able to purchase certain quantities of agricultural machinery imple- ments from the United States for 10 per cent less than the price at which he could be or is furnished with equivalent machinery by a home manufacturer. A Tariff Impost on the American machinery of 10 percent. or 15 per cent. will presumably force the farmer to purchase, at the higher prices, the British instead of the American implements. Obvi- ously he must cut down proportionately his expenditure in other directions. And, as obviously, he is thus compelled to deduct from the employment of other kinds of labour the 10 per cent. or 15 per cent. which he has 'been forced to contribute towards the employ- ment of the specially-protected British labourers who are employed in making agricultural machinery. WHO PAYS ?

As against this general Free-Trade argu- ment, Tariff Reformers constantly argue that

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There is quite a big bill at the Palace and Hippo- drome this week, and the management have their work cut out to crowd it all. into the allotted time. The:artiste who pleased us most was Eileen Douglas, a comedienne of the musical cc medy type possessing talent and charm above the ordinary. She sings with delightful humour and her dancing is .a poem in itself. Miss Douglas's song " The Musical Comedy Girl " as interpreted by herself is a remarkably good. number and makes one regret that encores are seldom permitted in the rush at two shows a night .houses. Quite a lot of time is taken up by the pretentious spectacular turn " Claude Duval" produced by Fred Ginnett and _ Wal Pink. Tttere.- are three acts very well staged, and a numerous company to form a setting for the exploits of the gallant highwayman, But there was a want of life and energy about the production which told against its success. Claude Duval's accomplished - . white horse was the 6nest performer. He did much and, said little, which the order was reversed in the case of most of the others. Some of the stilted speechify- ing. might well be cut, and the me!odrama will 1)e better when it has mellowed. Coverdale and Huxley's Juvenile Troupe excelled in a musical scena," Pansy's Birthday," and the songs, dances and jokes of these youthful performers were well received. Johnson and, Bert, cause much laughter with their novel act " A... Queer Customer," which is very actively sustained and never lags for an instant. The two performers possess both humour and originality, without which music-hall performers might as well take to coal- heaving. Another comedienne who realises the value of tuneful, clearly-enunciated songs backed by clever dancing is Ray .Ford, a young lady who deservedly got a good reception. •Some very fine animated pictures of life in the 'British Navy are shown on the oiosecpe, and Mr. Widdop's orchestra earns hearty appreciation for some .spirited selections and pleasing accompaniments. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. •

There is another capital bill for next week, the top place being given to Carney and Armstrong, comedians of a new school. Maurin's marvellous fountains will be a wonderful stage spectacle, over 20,090 gallons of Water pouring on the stage under powerful electric lights. Other attractions will be :—Ward and Davis, comedy artistes in " Wanted a Typist ; AMOS Hyward, coloured dancing comedian ' Jessie Wilton, chorus- comedienne ; Miss Norman Grant, society entertainer, at the piano ; Lola Patey, cnmedienne.

Thieves and Receivers Sentenced.

At Northwich, on Monday, Michael Comer and Martin Curly, of Warrington, and James Farran of Rochdale, were charged with the wholesale robbery of hams and bacon from the warehouse of George Hurst. Corner pleaded guilty, and was sentenced to three months' imprisonment, Curly was discharged, and Farran sentenced to one month. Francis Shaw and Walter Stockall were fined 5-1 each for receiving the stolen hams.

[By Redcap,]

POULTRY FOR EXHIBITION.

For those of my readers who have a fair amount of spare time and spare. capital together with a desire for an outdoor life I.know of no more 'interesting and profitable hobby than breeding poultry for exhibition if carried- out on the right lines. The growth in the number of poultry shows during the last twenty years has been enormous. There is scarcely a district Of any note in the British Isles but what has its annual show. I have also noticed .that of late, many horti- cultural societies have added poultry to their schedules, and it prompts one to congratulate those whose desire it is to create such healthy interest among the rural population of this country. . For the encouragement thus given not only tends to a more scientific education in producing pedigree stock, but also .to uplift the moral tope of the people in keeping the rural dweller more interested in the quality of his flowers and fowls than his beer. I appreciate the general 'interest that is taken in horticulture. I only wish that so many of the rural dwellers were as advanced -in scientific poultry farming.

TIIE BEGINNING of exhibition poultry farming generally settles whether the 'novice will continue or succeed, or whether after a series of losses he will give up in disgust, simply because he had no advice at the commencement. It is a great mistake to start poultry-keeping for exhibition purposes until a good general and theoretical knowledge is acquired. Sometimes one visit to a poultry show is sufficient to give you the Hen Fever " and you are apt to leave. the poultry 'pens with the idea that : " Well, they are only fowls. It must be easy enough to hatch a cup-winner out of a sitting of eggs." This is just what the writer himself did in the days of his youth, but with a continuation of .sore disappointments and a balance on the wrong side until a better knowledge was acquired. My advice to beginners is, to study the best works on poultry for exhibition, visit as often as is convenient the Shows, where_ you can coMpare the different varieties and their different points of merit, you are also able to get into, conversation with exhibitors who are always pleased to give you any information and advice. Fanciers are, generally speaking, a "good lot of chaps " and always willing to help a novice, remembering that they too have been novices. When you have thus far educated yourself and decided, on what breed to go in for—not forgetting to stick to One breed at least for a • start—get to know the best breeder of your special fancy and place yourself in his hands; tell him what you are prepared to' lay out for a. beginning and he u ill give you the best value accordingly. Many novices adopt wrong methods in relying on' thei r own limited knowledge. The follow ing are a few ERRORS

to be avoided. Don't purchase your stock at random, very often a good bird is picked up in a selling class at a show and mated to hens of another strain lltogether. They may be typical specimens but strain always counts and the novice often wonders how it is that he does not breed winners. I might say here that it is absolutely impossible to do so if such methods are adopted. Then again when a certain breed has been decided upon, good looking specimens of such breed are picked up here and there, without even a thought of how they are bred. My dear reader you must not hope to succeed on these lines. - Fanciers who have been most successful on the show bench have after long study of the different breeds built up a pedigree strain which is bound to throw exhibition stock. There are many points to watch in creating a standard in poultry. One of the most intricate is

===== 0002601_19090522_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
INBREEDING,

Although it . may seem against natural law to do so, it is essential in - breeding for a fixed type. All the • leading varieties of poultry have a standard of perfection to work to. And poultry fanciers rigidly observe these standards and endeavour to attain to such by methods of inbreeding, together with judicious and scientific crossing for certain points of improvement. And while -methods of inbreeding have to be resorted to in breeding for exhibition stock the same does not apply when breeding for utility purposes, simply because the result of close inbreed- ing means that a large percentage of the progeny will be weakly. That is why we do not get a pre- dominance of good exhibition specimens. So do not be disappointed if you only get one or two winners from -a sitting of eggs obtained from pedigree stocks. You may ask then is it advisable to purchase eggs for sitting or is it better to buy a small pen of birds and breed your own ? I unhesitatingly recommend that you should purchase stock and breed as. many from them as you can in one season. • Thus you will stand a better chance of obtaining a few good birds from what you have bred, rather than relying on hatching from eggs sent to you from birds that perhaps you have never seen. Not only so Imt you will already have the old stock birds for mating. again for another season which can be judiciously inbred with their progeny. This is the best way to create an exhibition stock Of your own, and if you enlarge your operations always do so with birds of the same strain, introduc- ing as new blood birds ,of a distant relationship. It always ,depends on your accommodation and sur- roundings as to what breed you should take up. Perhaps it would serve the purpose better if I mentioned-a few

===== 0002601_19090522_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
"VARIETIES

best adapted to certain conditions. Of course it depends again. on Your special taste, but when you find that your accommodation is not suitable for, the variety of your choice I-would persuade you to "fall in love" with some other variety if you would be successful. Where there is plenty of open space and cleanliness, birds of a light-coloured plumage may be kept, such as: Buff and White Orpingtons, Buff and White Leghorns, Buff and White Plymouth Rocks, Buff and White Cochins, Campine's, Faverolles, Buff, Silver and White Wyandottes, and Light Brah..ms. And for town runs and confined areas tirds of the darker plumage are better, such as Andalusians, AncOnas; Black, Brown and Blue (a new breed) Leghorns, -Black and Spangled Orpingtons, Black and Barred Plymouth Rocks, Partridge and Black Cochins, Dark Bi:aliams, Dorkings, Old English. Game, Hamburghs, Houdans, Indian Game, ('road and Modern Langshans, Black Minorcas, Spanish, Sumatra Game, Partridge, Black, Silver Laced, Silver Pencilled Black Laced and Blue Wyandottes. I think I have given you sufficient to choose from but I have ,y no means exhausted the whole of the different varieties of poultry. And I would not conclude this article Without saying a word in favour of the many varieties of

===== 0002601_19090522_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
DXNTAMS

who do yeoman service in bringing money into the pockets of their many .succeesful breeders.. I :have personally seen a Black Rosecomb Bantam Cockerel change hands at the substantial figure of ;(230 ,and I know a gentleman in the Bantam fancy who once sold a Pile Game Bantam for £5O. So you see there is money in the "mites" of the poUltry world and to 'anyone whose space is limited keeping bantams for exhibition purposes is a profitable hobby. It may be of _interest if I name a few of the different varieties of Bantams. If you have never visited • one Of our large poultry shows you can have no idea of the importance these interesting little birds create to the authorites who rim the show. More often than not the Bantam Classes are full and help to make these

show fixtures a success. There are almost numberless varieties, the chief of which are, Black Red, Blue Red, Brown Red, Duckwing and Spangle, Old English Game. The same colours also exist in the Modern Game. I ought to mention that the difference between an Old English and a inedern Game is that the former are cobby and short on leg, while the latter are more racy and higher on leg. Sometimes they look as if walking on stilts. - Then in the soft- feathered breeds of Bantams we have Black Rosecomb,. White Rosecomb, Gold and Silver Sebrights, Part- ridges; Black, Buff, and White Pekins, Scotch Greys, Light and Dark Brahmin Bantams, Japanese, Polish, Black and White Booted, Malay, Indian Game. Ilamburgh and Wyandotte Bantams are now also being extensively bred and improved. There are others which I need hardly mention here. Some may wonder how they have been produced. It has been done by a process of inbreeding and late-breeding,- in fact any method which would retard growth and bantamise consistent with a humane system. All that should be said in connection with poultry for exhibition is not possible in this -.column, so I shall next week detail their management.

OPEN-AIR POLITICS.

PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE'S CAMPAIGN.

Mr. Haws an Mining F.oyaititn.

The Warrington Progressive Labour League has arranged a series of open-air meetings for the summer months,and the campaign was successfully inaugurated on Monday evening with a meeting at Bridge Foot (near Arpley Station). There was a large attendance and the-speeches were enthusiastically received. Mr. George Wallington presided and was supported by Messrs. Joseph Howes, E. Wynne Jones, S. Wright, J. A. J. Morton and J. Howes, jun. (hon. secretaries of the League).

The Csi;siititset said he was a convinced Free Trader and had been all his life. The difficulty to him was how any man of intelligence could propose to wake it worse for us to compete with foreign countries. We were a manufacturing nation, we had sand our goods all over the world, and our ships were on every sea. If we made it dearer to buy our goods as they came into th;s country, how in the name of commonsense catild we sell our th'ngs as cheaply as we were now able to do in all the neutral maikets ? That was a question which he had never seen answered by any tariff reformer who hid been to the fore during the last four years. Protect7on was not a new idea. It had been promulgated and thrashed °et for the last 100 years, and it had been condemned by all people who had thought seriously about it and g ne into the question. If tariff reform were adopted, the present temporary rise in the re ices of feod—dits to natural causes—would be permanent. Self-intsrest and ignorance bad a great deal te do with tariff reform advocacy. He admitted that the policy might he a good thing temporarily for a few interssted parties, but he was convinced that the better course for the co', ntry at large was to follow the 'present economic system of buying in the cheapest market and selling in the dearest. Tariffs would, in the long ron,instead of improving" our position make it iefiaitely worse. The countries which had Protection were not as well off as we were to-day. We had passed through tad times, but they had been no'hing to comsare with the conditions in America. 'i here were more thowands walking the streets ited starving there than there were in this decrepit old country, as some would call it. We could not do better, concluded Mr. Wallington, than follow on the lines adopted by cur fathers fifty years ago, and enjoy the inheritance which they had handed down. (Applause.) Mr. E. Wve-s;f4 JONES said it was good to be alive at the present time and it was a grand thing to be a Liberal. A splendid Budget had been introduced by Mr. Lloyd-George who was trying to put the burden on the right shoulders and make those people pay who were best able to do so. Liberals did not stand simply for the maintenance of Free Trade, but also for the freedom of the land. (Applause.) He could not under- stand the great aversion that the tariff reformers seemed to have to taxing raw materials. They said they would only tax foodstuffs and that the foreigner would pay the tax. If that were so, why did they not go in for taxing raw materilds as well? Why not let the foreigner pay the lot ? The fact teas that under tariff reform the levying of taxes would increase and if they commenced by taxing foodstuffs they would finish by taxing raw materials. If they started they would have to go the whole hog—they would even have to , tax the hustles on the hog's back. (Laughter.) We were told that some sacrifice must be male in I order to save the Empire. He sub.nitted that we had made sufficient sacrifices already for the Colonies. Why should we endanger our trade with' our best customers for purely imaginary better relations with the Colonies? It was the fiscal policy of tte Colonies that stood in the way of closer relationships, and not the Free Trade policy of the neither cauntry. Protection was vicious in principle, and we must be more that we did not adopt it, for it would load to all kinds of trickery and dodging. It did so in those countries st, here it already obtained, and if Protection ever came to pass in this land it would be a means of creating a class that would became the willing con- tributors to corruption funds. Free Trade made against international rival' ies and jealonsie-,. It made for, brotherly relations the woild over, but a Protectionist system had the opposite effect. I.et.ns maintain Free Trade—the system which had made this country what it a as, which had raised it to the position it, now enjoyed amongst the nations of the world. Land was the primary element in the production and distribution of wealth, and land shoold go into the mirket at Free Trade prices for those men who could put it to the best possible we. Our present land system hindered, cramedi, and cotfined trade, and Liberals would not be satisfied until we had got freedom of the land as well as of trade. (Applause). • Mr. HOWES, who had a cordial reception, said it was the intention of the League to hold weekly ire-Alves , and it was hopel the, speakers would include Mr. Neilson, Mr. Fen eick and Mr. Ward. Anti-Hug to last we-k's open air meeting in connection with the Tariff Reform League, he said the Chairman (Councillor Broadhurst) stated that " th's country was the most favourably placed country in the world, end it might manufacture the whole of the raw material required— over £20,00,000 of iron and steel." The answer to that was very short and pointed. Yes, we might, if the Tories would help us to take off the Tory tax on our iron and steel. It was not the foreigner who punished us ; it was the royalties—(applauss)—the plunder tal;en by the landlords, which was put en by the Tory Government years ago and which had been 'kept on by the Tory party. Mr. Lloyd George was the first Chancellor of the Exchequer who had (red to interfere with that great monoply. (Applause). Another speaker at the Tariff Reform meeting wss a schoolmaster (Mr. Martin) who began by telling them ttat he w a not a politician—and his speech pro% ed it (laughter). The man NA ho cared nothing for politics was not doing his duty as a citizen, because it was the duty of every man to to interested in the form of government that con- trolled the sff4irs of the nation. This gentleman said that he intended to vote for the party that would give tariff reform, and by chance it happened to be the Unionist party. (Leugliter.) No, not by chance but by a used determination. By chance it could not ho the Liberal party or the Labour patty, because every Labour representative in the Hotr-e of Commons was a Free Trader. (Applause.) The great Tory leader, Lord Beaconsfield, said Protection was damned - a rather forcible expression. (Laughter) Mr. Martin said "he did not say that tariff reform would cure all unemploy- ment"—well that, was an improvement because a few months ago they declared that it would—" but it would cure all the unemployment which was caused by foolish tariffs." Which were the foolish tariffs and which were

the v.ise ones? The tariff reformers were always pre:. testing against the foreigner. Who was the foreigner) A man who happened to be born where we were not and who could not help it. (Laughter.) If it was wicked for the foreigner to send us goods it was equally wicked for us to send our goods abroad ; if it was a Lad thing t a receive goods -from other countries it was equa!ly had for us to make things and send them to other countries, so we had better build a wall round, shut ourselves in and sing " Glory for ever" and starve to death. (Laughter.) Mr. Martin spoke.of the question of wag': and said cheap production did not depend upcn low wages. (Applause.) Referring to the Budget, Mr. Howes said they were in for one of the greatest fights they had ever been engaged in—the fight on the land question, (Applause.) The Government was going to stimulate trade by taxing royalties and land values and calling upon the great monopolists to liberate the raw material and the land. He would Me . to give them one of the most striking instances of and - and royalty monopoly that could possibly be placed before them. When the "Lusitania " was under fall' full steam she consumed 70 tons of coal per hcur,which was equal to 1 ten 3cNyt. 371bs. per minute, and 1.60 tons every day of 24 honrs. The landlord got naore as royalty in one hour than the trimmer received i 9 wages for 24 hours work ' • the royalty for one &Y. equalled 2 years wages and the royalty on the round LOP equalled 20 years wages of the trimmer. For every pent.v the trimmer received as wages the landlord gel' 58s. 41. The difference in social value of the landlord and coal trimmer was 70,000 per cent. They ought to have stared a patrii tic find, and allowed those who wanted eight to plank down the money for eight. PROSPECTS.

A Tariff Reform Object Lesson.

NOTES ON GAMES, ETC.

Cricket.

If Warrington maintain the splendid form which has characterised their play thus far, they should have a flue record to show at the end of the season. On Saturday they played their first • League match at Arpley and proved superior to: Prescot in a marked degree. Interest in the town eleven's doings is evidently on the increase, for nearly four hundred spectators assembled, and there is little doubt that if the Warringtonians make a determined bid for League honours—as they seem likely to 4.9—they will receive considerably more support than is usually accorded. The visitors had first turn at the wicket and their Venture realized 107. IL Callon gave the batsmen a great deal of trouble and came out with thè. creditable analysis of seven wickets for 33 runs. W. A. Ashall was able to resist all attempts to disturb his pegs and carried out his bat with 33 to his account.

Warrington replied with a grand display,-and at 173 for 7 the innings was closed. W. Richardson and J. Callon were the first pair in, and the start was enCouraging. Callon was in a meriy mood and seored five fours and a single off the first two overs. He had knocked up .16 before his partner opened his account. Both batsmen bit out in dashing style and fifty was the total after fifteen minutes' play. Richardson was smartly caught at 10 by Holden and T. Wright took his place. The aggregate reached 66 before a separa- tion was effected, Callon being caught at the wicket by Mercer. His innings of 41 was a magnificent per- formance and included eight fours.

T. Wright was also in brilliant form and the score_ rapidly mounted up. W. H. Podmore who had joined him added seven when he was caught at cover point. B. Silcock, the captain, then had a turn and some attractive play was witnessed, each batsman be7ng in tip-top form,and the bowling was repeatedly changed.. At 133 Wright's innings came to a close, Mercer holding a catch at the wicket. The retiring batsman haa compiled 39 in faultless style. Shortly afterwards Silcock's effort concluded and he is deserving of equal praise for his 38. From this stage little interest centred in the game. J. W. Bainbridge was caught in the long field for 16 and S. Partridg batted well for his not-out innings of 18..

Warrington 2nd also acquitted .themselves brilli- antly at Prescot for after dismissing the home side for 95, they amassed 156 for, the loss of seven wickets A. E. Walker batted ,in grand style and placed 45 to his credit. Waywell and Bennett shared the bowling, their respective analyses being three wickets for 46 runs and seven wickets for 47 runs. Jones bowled effectively for the home team, capturing five wickets for 29 runs.

There were two other matches in each division of the West Lancashire League. St. Helens Recs. defeated Newton by 142 to 118 and Earlestown accounted for St. Helens by 115 to 97. In the second division, the victories rested with the same clubs, St. Helens Recs. piling up 189 for 7, against Newton's 100, while Earlestown trounced St. Helens by 225 to

The Warrington and District League competition is being entered into with great zest and so far St. James', the champions, are maintaining their old form. On Saturday they met Walker's Brewery and triumphed by 22 runs. J. Hallam (26 not out) and C. Holt (23) batted wall and Holt was also in great form with the ball, capturing seven wickets for 25 runs. For Walker's W. Rylance knocked up a capital 38,and J. Savage performed the `: hat trick.

Sankey Wire Mills entertained Whitecrrss Rees., and ran out winners by 15. There was no tall scoring, \V. H. Bell heading the list with 13.

Wilderspool Brewery were -victorious at the expense of the " Guardian " office, the majority being 39. For the winners W. Howard (28) and T. Thompson (1.6)

were the MDBt. useful bat-,Lnan, while Howard and Lloyd bowled very well.

Latchford Baptists administered defeat to nylands Bros. to the extent of 28. E. Harris distinguished himself with a fine innings for 43, while for the losers G. McGowan accounted for eight wickets for 23.

There were three matches in the Warrington and District Junior League. How-ley visited Stretton and returned triumphant-by 17. R. Gifford (18) wis the top scorer, and Wilcocks took five wickets for seven runs.

t'St. Mary's were severely trounced by Holy Trinity who got them all olt for 25 and then amassed the fine total of 142. T. Worthington wielded the willow in dashing style, coppiling 57, and Carmen to3k six wickets for eight runs. • •

Sankey entertained Orford and a low scoring match resulted in a win for the home eleven by eight runs. E. Foster and A, Clare each made 10 for the winners and losers respectively.

Warrington S3conlary 5:..h00l gave a capital account of themselves at Widnes, defeating the Secondary School of the chemical centre by 46 runs. Parsonage (21), Stirrup (20 not out) and Reddish (23) gave smart batting displays.

Latchford Baptists met WinwiCk Asylum eleveli and beat them to the tune of 45 runs. A. Albinson (19) and J. Cox (18) were the most successful bats- men.

In their match with Mothre and Daresbury, Sankey Wire Mills won by 2 runs with 5 wickets to spare. J. L. Hawthorn was the highest scorer with 19, and IL Gandy made a useful 14 for the visitors.

Walker's Brewery were successful in their encounter with Manchester Brewery. After dismissing their opponents for 52 they piled up the score of 92 for the lo3s of 8 wickets. W. Wright and J. Cain were the chief scorers, making 28 and ,21 respectively. A. Holland also bowled well, taking 6 wickets for 19 runs.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

The remarkable popularity which this healthful sport has achieved of late years shows no signs of waning this season,on the contrary the various competi- tions are being taken up with even greater enthusiasm. There were four matches in the Warrington Works competition, the successful teams being Monks, Hall No. 2, Corporation, Fletcher's .No. 1 and Fletcher's No. 3.

Monks, Hall and Co. No. 2, after conceding E. Forshaw and Son five points, defeated them by 3.3. One of the inost interesting contests was between T. Bells (Forshaw's) and H. Deakin (Monks's.) Bells started well, and at the tenth end had the comfortable lead of 9-4. Deakin improved, and for some time the game was of a give-and-take character. The players peeled at 12, and then Bellis got ahead and ran out winner by 15-12.

Another exciting encounter was between J, Thomas (Monks's captain) and T. Lawton (Forshaw's). Until almost the close it look<=d 'pretty certain that Lawton would be the victor and at the fourteenth end he had the advantage by 12-6. Then he could make no further .progress and Thomas scoring consistently won by 15-12.

Corporation were allowed 5 start of P. & K. who are such a formidable team that they were placed on scratch. The result was a win for the Corporation by a margin of nine points.

There were several closely-fought games, two of the best matched opponents being R. Rothwell (P. & K.) and J. Cooper (Corporation). They peeled at 6, 11, and 14, and then Cooper had the good fortune to bowl the winning wood which gave him the victory by - the odd point. Another keen str*le was that between D. Gibson (P. & K.) and T. Dawson (Corporation). At the start Gibson had a run of successes and led 6-1. Dawson then found his form and the players were equal at 7, 11, and 13. Then Dawson notched a. point but -Gibson went one better and triumphed by 15-11

Fletcher's No. 1, demonstrated their superiority over Robertson's in unmistakable fashion for allowing their opponents 35 points, they ran out winners by 28. There were not many games of exceptional ikerest. The closest was between R. T. Dutton (Robertson's) and T. Lampart (Fletcher's). The con- test ints appealed at 10,12 and 14, and then Dutton achieved success by a single point.

Fletcher's No.' 3 bad doughty opponents in Bifurcated Rivet. The teams started on equal terms, aud the Gas Engineers won by the small margin of 4 points. There was a notable contest between J. Shelmerdine (Rivet Works) and J. llohnes (Fletcher's). Shehnerdine had all the success for a song time and led by as much as 11-5. Holmes, however, gradually made headway, and the players were level at 13 and 14, Shelmerdine then bowling the winning wood, .1. Roberts (Rivet) and F. Singleton (Fletcher's) a 1,.) provided a capital game. There was never much between them, and they peeled at 9 and 13. Roberts then placed two to his credit, and won by 15-13.

The first round of the Warrington and District Association Championship was opened on Saturday with three matches. The surprise of he afternoon was the defeat of Warrington Cemetery by Whitecross at Earlestown. The wire men held th 4 upper hand throughout the match and won by a mq.jority of 10 points. The bowling g- enerally was good' and the game between J. Slavin and G. Ball, jun., was particularly keen. Slavin was in great form and finished with a margin of 6 points to his credit.

Bull's Head met Earlestown at Runcorn and gained the victory. by 18 points. A splendid game Was that between 0. Ithubotham and AV. Andrews, the first- named winning by a single point:

Singularly enough, llylands' No. 1 had lands' No. 2 for their opponents and the result was a triumph for No. Iby 52 points. F. Miller and W. Norgrove were exceptionally well matched, Miller winning by 21-20.

DEATH OF MR. WILLIAM CROSFIELD

Tragic End at a Charity Meeting.

Many Warrington people , Nvil 1 deeply regret to hear of the death of Mr. William Crosfield, J.P., of Liverpool, which took place with tragic suddenness on Monday. Mr. Cros- field was one of a .party of ladies and gentlemen who 'responded to the Lord Mayor's invitation to attend an informal conference at the Town Hall to consider what might be done locally with regard to the co-ordination of charitable agencies, in accordance with the recom- mendations in the Majority Report of the Poor Law Commission: Mr. Crosfield, who appeared to be in good health, and who seemed to have fully recovered from the operation which he underwent a few months back, rose to speak shortly before five- o'clock. His address was perfectly lucid and was delivered with all his characteristic fervour and fluency, Mr. Crosfield sat down somewhlt flushed and excited. Two minutes later, whilst another speaker was hold- ing the attention of the conference, he collapsed in his seat. He had had an apoplectic seizure and despite medical attention, passed away in a few moments.

'emission of the" Daily News." Photo Dirties. 1E LATE MR. WILLIAM CROSFIELD, J.P.

Mr. Crosfield was a native of Liverpool and was born in 1838. After receiving his education at the Royal Institution, Colquitt-street, which ceased to be a teaching academy many years ago, he entered his fa!her's business—that of George Crosfield and Co., sugar refiners—being soon admitted a partner, and 011 the death of his father, in 1881, became the head of the firm. It was said of his father, who in the early part of last century was in business in Warrington, that he acquired great wealth blamelessly. When he died he left a considerable fortune, and as Mr. Wm, Crosfield inherited a substantial portion of it, he was no doubt moved to relax his attention to business, the conduct of which he 'left to capable partners, and devoted himself to religious and social work. When little more than a youth he began to devote himself with great energy to the .Liberal cause, and throughout his life he remained a staunch advocate of progress and reform. 1n.1883 he was elected to the City Council and served on that body with assiduity and ability. In the meantime, Mr. Crosfield had been adopted as Parliamentary candidate for the borough of Warrington, a town with which his family was closely connected, and, consequently, he did not seek re-election for the Council at the end of term of service there.. At this time he lived at- Waterloo, and took a keen interest in the political life of the newly-created Southport Division. At the great general election of 1885, the first after the redistribution of seats, he made a gallant fight for Liberalism at Warrington, but Sir Gilbert Greenall proved too strong for him, and he was defeated by 776 votes. The, following year he contested Lincoln city as a Gladstonian Home Ruler, but was again beaten, this time by Mr. F. H. Kerans by a majority of 308. Undeterred, he resolved to win the city for Liberalism, and during the next few years devoted much time to propaganda and organisation work there, with'the result that in 1892 lie had the gratification of finding himself at the head of die poll, with a majority over his opponent, Mr. Kerans, of 224. . He spent the next three years in close attendance at the House of Ominous, and was a gogd deal missed in many Liverpool circles. His Parliamentary career was a short one, though he made the best of his opportunities while it lasted— for at the general election of 1893 Lincoln returned to the Tory faith, and rejeeted Mr. Crosfield in favour of Mr. C. H. Seely .(eldest brother of Colonel Seely, M.P.), who stood as an uncomprising Unionist, and Secured the:narrOw 'majority of 218. Mr. Crogfield -re-entered the City Council in 1910 and served until 1906 When he Was defeated by Mr. G. F. Clarke, of Earlestown, on the Education 'question. He was a city magistrate, a member of the Select Vestry, the Mersey Dock Board and of the 'Liverpool Education Committee. He was a devoted Congregationalist and his connection with the county Congregational organisation and with the Congre- gational' Union of England and Wales has been of the closest. Until this month, when he resigned, he occupied the position of treasurer of the Union. He worked hard for Mansfield College. Oxford, and his power and purse have ever been used freely for the exteasion of Congregational principles. Another of his many cares was the great London Missionary Society, in the interest of which, as well as for the benefit of his Own health, he a few years ago accom- panied the Rev. Dr. Wardlaw Thompson, the general secretary, on a tour of visits to the mission' stations in the Pacific Islands, -

As a mark of respect to the deceased gentleman the flag at the Warrington Liberal Club was displayed at half mast.

IMPRESSIVE FUNERAL.

The widespread esteem and affection in which Mr. William Crosfleld was_held by all sections of the com- munity was manifested on Thursday, when his remains were reverently laid to rest in Smithdown-road Cemetery. Pr:or to the interment a memorial service was held in Great George-street. Chapel, in the presence of a numerous attendance, rept.. sentative of the City Council, the Dock Board, and religions, political, poor- law, and charitable bodies w ith which the deceased gentleman had been associated. The Rev. R. Herbert Sewell (minister of the church) officiated, and was assist el by the Rev. Dr. Goodrich, of ! Manchester (ex-chairman of the Congregational Union of England and Wales), and the Rev. John Yonge, of Warrington (representing the Lancashire Congrega- tional Union). On the platfor.n in the church, repre- senting the various Congregational churches, were the Revs. G. E. Cheeseman (Huyton) and A.. 1. Viner, rep re - seating the Lancashire Congregatioaal Union; G. A. Brock (Waterloo), C. F. Richardson (Wigan), A. A. Lee (Bootle), J. E. Neil (Hoylake), 0. L. Roberts (Welsh Tabernacle), Elwyn Thomas (Norwood), W. M. Kawlin- son (Staele), J. Methieson Person (Crescent), F. W. Nicholson (Chedwiik-mount), W. L. Roberts (Harting- ton-road), W. Eaton Thomas (I.iscard), J. A. Middleton (Prenton), and the Rev. F. Barnes ' • also the Rev. Alexander Connell (Sefton Park Presbyterian Church). '1 he hymn "0 God, our help in ages past " was sung by the congregation, and the Rev. Dr. Goodrich having read the Scripture, Mr. Sewell delivered an impressive and eloquent address in the course of which he said there never breathed a man more averse to adalation, more unrerppesive to the language of idle compliment, than William Crosfield. He was that rare and beautiful combination, a publle man who shrink from publicity ; a powerful speaker, who preferred silence to words; a man of war, to whom the ways of peace were inexpres- sibly dearer than the clash of arms. He was in the hest tense of the word a nein, standing firm upon his feet, those feet planted, not upon the shifting sands of expediency, hut upon the solid rock of pi iaeipte ; stsnding fuer square to ell the winds that blew. Strong men are sometimes lacking in the softer qualities ; the man who indomitably shoulders his own burden is now and then indifferent to the burdens of others. The crown- ing glory of the character of William Crosfield WAS its combination of the gentler qualities wit h the more robust Ho was alsrays giving money, his days, his skill, his common sense, and, most and best of all, himself. His life was built into the life of his Church, which owes more to 11:m than perhaps ever Church oxved to man before. It eas built into the life ef his denomination. many of whose highest officers have journeyed here to-day to attest Congregational appre- ciation of his services. It. was built into the life of his party, whose vthale-souled champion he has been through evil report and good. It was freely at the service of the poor and the distressed. His last act was the pleading of their ciltSEI ; the best of his dsys were expended in the ministry to their needs. The tropical islands of the Southern Ocean were not too far to be reached by his glence of pity and his 'tend of help ; his master enthusiasm vp.s that of the Christian Imperialism which would, extend the blessed rule of the Redeemer to the uttermost cads of the earth

So he lived, and so he died, and so we dull remember

The route to the cemetery was lined with silent spectators. Here a large body of rnoaraers had gathered, and the committal service was conducted in the presence of many moistened eyes. Th 3 band and a. detachment of boss from the training-ship Inde fat igable were in attendance under C tptain Bremner. and they rendered the beautiful, if me!ancholy, hymn, ." Days and moments qui.:kly flying" with great feeling.

who do its work. The worst of it is that, granted a system of payment of Members, adequate safeguards against abuse are not easy to invent. People ask, and with some reason, whether or not the attachment of a salary to a seat would pot bring forward in our midst the type of a Yankee leather- lunged, self-seeking politician "on the make." In France it is claimed payment of members has produced members who think of nothing else but payment, and who are at the service of any man with an axe to grind who will supplement their salaries with bribes. Cer- tainly it would be a deplorable thing were the tone of our public life to be lowered : it would 'scarcely be worth while to do that even to approach more nearly to that desir- able thing "equality of opportunity." We should, therefore, examine with very great care proposals for a system of maintenance. ' Should such a system be decided upon, it would be well to make the salaries so small as not to be very tempting t o rascals. Also it would not be a very bad thing to take all possible precautions against the size of the salaries being increased. All these considerations will probably have to be weighed very carefully in the near future, as well as the consideration whether this proposal is not (albeit most of its supporters do not know it) the thin end of an exceedingly large wedge. Already in political holes and corners daring persons are actually advocating the payment of members of towns councils and district councils. Many men would stop short at that who would favour payment of M.P.'s on its own merit. Little of what we have pointed out as argu- ment against State payment of salaries applies to that other thing which is generally bracketed with it—State payment of election expenses. Election expenses, coming as they do in a heavy lump, are a very great burden on poor men. Their payment by the Exchequer would relieve candidates without offering an inducement to undesirable candi- dates to come forward, as there would be nothing to be "got out" of it.

. Warrington is pulsating THE ROYAL with an ebullition of VISIT. loyalty at the prospect

of the visit Of their Majesties the King and Queen on Tuesday, July 6th. Such an event in the old borough will be unique and the townspeople can be relied upon to make the most of so glorious ..an occasion. Our gracious King and. Queen have won the love and loyalty of all their subjects and fully maintained the high and beneficent standard of monarchi- cal government set up during the long reign of Victoria the Good. Warrington appre- ciates the signal honour and privilege of welcoming Edward the Peacemaker and Alexandra the Tenderhearted. Their stay may be brief, but it will make a deep and lasting impression on the hearts of thousands of Warringtonians, young and old. Between now and the eventful day the hours and the minutes will be impatiently counted.

People whose interest THE FOOTBALL in football is of the CLUB. most casual order pro- bably do not realise that the Warrington Football Club is one of the livest and most democratic institutions in the town. It has an annual turnover of over £4,000 and a paid membership of over 1,000 and these members one and all take the keenest personal interest in the welfare of the Club. Should failure happen to be the dominant note of a season's doings—as it was during the season just concluded—then the annual meeting takes the form of a dis- turbed bee-hive and the poor hardworking, unpaid Committee gets severely handled. One would think that a seat on such'a body would have little attraction to anyone, but the competition for places is as keen as if there were a comfortable living attached. This is 'one of the best features of the Club. Dozens of men—and busy men too—are willing to sacrifice a great deal of time and labour in order that the Football Club shall maintain its proud position in the realm of Rugby, knowing at the same time that they are quite likely to be metaphorically kicked for their pains a year later. The annual meeting held. on Thursday evening was clearly illus- trative of all these things. Com- mitteemen were belaboured, but they clung to their positions, and it says much for the good sense of the members that notwithstanding a strong feeling of dissatis- faction they retained all but one of the retiring managers, and contented themselves with enlarging the numbers from six to eight and in this way introducing the required amount of new blood. The Club is in a strong position in every respect, and it only requires enterprise, discrimination, and the power to act quickly, on the part of the new management for the reproaches of the past to be wiped out by the achievements of the future.

Mr. Churchill's bill to LEGISLATING FOR set up Labour UNEMPLOYMENT. Exchanges promises a substantial measure of, reform in unemployment. It is intended to come into force at the beginning of next year and it willfbe followed, after a short interval, by a scheme for compulsory insurance against unemployment. The bill introduced on Thursday proposes a system whose principal outlines May be readily indicated. In towns both large and small offices will be set up at which applicants in search of work may register, and not only will all the demands and provisions for local employment come within the purview of the directors,but every exchange will be in touch with a central clearing house, and with all similar agencies up and down the country. An applicant, if he does not find work that he can do in his own county, may find it in the,next. A clearing house will be set up for each of the ten divisions into which the country will be divided. There will be forty labour exchanges in the larger towns, forty-five in the smaller, and 150 on a minor scale in the less populous centres. The advisory com- mittees in the big centres will keep in touch with the trade unions, and will consist of tepresentatives of employers and employed in equal numbers, under the presidency of an independent civil official. While the building programme' is going forward the scheme will cost £200,000 a year; ordinarily it will be £30,000 less. The project, fulfilling, at once the main recom- mendation on this subject of both the Majority and Minority reports of the Poor Law Commission, will go a considerable way towards permanently dealing with the problem of unemployment. From one point of view, the most important promise in the fore- cast of the Government's insurance scheme is that the interests of voluntary agencies wil

be fully safeguarded. The scheme will be tripartite. The individual payment of six- pence a week will be contributed by work- man, . employer, And Government jointly, and in practical working the two remedies together will ensure that a deserving man applying at an exchange will get work if there be any, or, if there be none, the benefit fo which he is entitled by his payments against unemployment. The schemes hold together well, and between Old-Age Pensions on the one hand, and the Development Fund on the other, there are the best of reasons for believing that this ancient evil will at last be found to yield to scientific treatment.

Lord Onslow, it is to be THE PROGRESS OF presumed, did not SMALL HOLDINGS, intend to offer to the President of the Board of Agriculture a guitable opportunity for contrasting the good work of the Liberal Government with the failure of the Con- servative Small Holdings Act. But that was the net result of his inquiries. Last week, Lord Carrington- pointed out, the Govern- ment had had submitted to them schemes and orders dealing with 1,256 acres. That was fifty per cent, more land in one week under the new Act than had been dealt with in fifteen years under the old. Very recently we had Mr. Jesse Collings parading the ancient catch-word of "the magic of property," and denouncing the Government because it sought to create occupiers of small holdings rather than owners. But, as Sir Edward Strachey showed in his statement in the House of Commons last week, of all the English appli- cants for small holdings, only I'6 per cent 'expressed a desire to purchase. The Liberal Government have given the applicant for a small holding what he really wants; the Conservative Government would only give him what, in their opinion, they think he ought to want. But the chief reason in this astonishing contrast is that the Liberal measure was meant to work, and the Con- servative measure was only meant for show.

The Opposition attack made upon Mr. Haldane _ _

"BRAZEN

CAP-TRAP." in the -House of Lords

provoked a scathing rejoiner from Lord Lucas, which was certainly not undeserved. Lord Roberts, who spoke of "the deplorable condition of the Army," expressed his astonishment that the Army should be made a Party question. While the Opposition is ready to exploit Lord Roberts's name and prestige, it is certainly not ready to endorse his panacea—universal compulsory training. And it was a Conservative Secretary of State who dismissed Lora Roberts from the War Office with less consideration—in the gallant commander's own phrase—than would be given to an office-boy. The Opposition has not only made the Army a Party Question; it has • stooped to surround it with per- sonalities which 'degrade the House of Lords to the level of a pot-house brawl. Lord Newton explained away the con- fidence of the public in the present adminis- tration of the War Office, as the delusion of simple-minded people bewildered by Mr. Haldane's "brazen clap-trap." Lord Lucas retorted that when the Liberal Government came into office they found the Army in a state of absolute break-down, and if the Commission for which the Opposition asked should ever sit,it would be more likely to demand the heads of noble lords on the Opposition benches than those of a Govern- ment which had set on foot two such useful and important institutions as the special reserve and the officers' training corps.

NAND N TIONS.

Those of our readers who are interested in our weekly Column on poultry farming will be concerned to hear of a man who cheer- fully loses a hundred and fifty a year at poultry-farming. It is worth it, he says, for the work takes all the stuffing out of a shrewish wife !" * * _

Does anybody in these degenerate days read the vigorous novels of Harrison Ains- worth? The question is suggested by an appeal for funds to restore the ancient parish church at Whalley, which figures so pro- minently ill the Lancashire Witches?' Apart from architectural merit as a fine specimen 'of the Early English style, this interesting old edifice, which has suffered sadly at different periods from fire and "restorers," possesses some valuable art- treasures. Among them are beautifully carved choir-stalls, which were rescued from ruined Whalley Abbey when the last Abbot was hanged by Henry VIII. for joining in the "Pilgrimage of Grace." * * *

The pension which Sir John Gorst has just voluntarily relinquished is worth £1,200 a year. Sir John has held it since August 11th, 1902. It is what is known as a second- class political pension. The qualification is service of not less than six years in a political office the salary of which is less than £5,000 and over £2,000 a year, but the service need not have been continuous nor all in one office. There can only be four of these pensions in existence at the same time, and till Sir John Gorst relinquished his there was no vacancy. The other pensions of this class are held by Mr. Chaplin, Lord Balfour, of Burleigh, and Mr. Gerald Balfour.

* * There are three classes of political pensions, and they are given under an Act which was passed in 1869. A first-class pension is worth £2,000 a year, but an appli- cant must have seived for four years in a political office thb salary of which is not less than £5,000 a year. Only four of these pensions have been granted since the Act was passed—to the late Mr. Childers, to the late Lord Iddesleigh, to Lord Cross, and to Lord George Hamilton. Lord Cross and Lord Geor4e Hamilton are still drawing their pensions, but the other two of the four available have not yet been granted. The first pension given under the Act was to the celebrated Mr. Villiers ; it was £1,200 a year but he enjoyed it many years, and drew in all £30,810. .

* * After the afternoon session of the Women's National Liberal Association on Tuesday, the delegates made their way to 8, Hertford- street, Mayfair, where Mrs. Joseph Pease entertained them for over an hour with tea and music. About three hundred guests were present at Mrs. Pease's invitation, including Mrs. Asquith, Miss Haldane, Mrs. Whitley, Mrs. Herbert Lewis, Lady Havelock-Allan, Mrs. Alfred • Illingworth, Mrs. McKenna, Mrs. Henry, Hon. Mrs. E. M. Dunne, Mrs. W. P. Byles, Lady Barran, and Lady Edwards. After the music of Miss Joicey, Miss Edwards, Miss Emmott, and Mrs. Crosfield, Mrs. Asquith gave a short message of goodwill from her husband to the women who were working throughout the country for Liberalism.

* * The process of planting trees at the Garden City of Letchworth goes on apace. Some- thing like one hundred thousand trees have now been planted, merely for ornamental

purpose's. The general scheme (says the Liverpool Post ") is to identify each street with a particular kind of tree; but, of course as the city extends its bounds this will be increasingly difficult. There is something to be said for the planting of four thousand fir trees in the more industrial portion of the estate, in order that the factories may be hidden as much as possible from the dwell- ings, though the Letchworth factories deserve this indignity less than any other factories in the country. Abutting on the streets there are fruit trees, and the children are trained to abstain from touching them on any account. It is declared that the chil- dren respond to the appeal which is thus made to their honour, and it is very rarely that .the rule is infringed. Twenty years hence Letchworth will be a vision of glory, and that is not to infer that it is short of being beautiful to-day. o _ _ _ _

Incidentally, it ee--ms likely that the Children Act will prpvide a new occupation for the professional loafer. Outside a public- house the other day, a correspondent 9f the "Manchester Guardian" writes, I saw, a 'father, mother, and child of four. The parents looked wistfully at the public-house and doubtfully at the child, with an expres- sion which said, "What can we do .with her." A keen-eyed loafer detached himself quickly from the public-house wall and said, You go in; I'll look after 'er for yer." * *

Rostherne will be the scene of another demonstration to-day, bat in addition to being banned the demonstrators will find themselves barred. Lord Egerton is inclined to show fight and has fenced in the field which served .for a meeting place on the occasion of the first protest. Latest reports say that last night the gates and fences were to b 3 tarred, as an additional deterrent. Nevertheless the demonstration will doubt- less b 3 held. His Lordship is rather unwise to introduce tar into the controversy. Opponents may retaliate ..wjth tar and feathers if they can find a suitable medium. We suppose however that Lord Egerton will again be specially absent for the occasion. * * _

Mr. Joseph Havelo3k Wilson, M.P., who will address an open air meeting promoted by the Warrington Progressive Labour League on Monday evening next, has had a varied career, including an apprenticeship to printing and some years experidnce as a sea- man which took him to the coasts of Australia and America and various foreign parts. He is the founder and President of the National Seamen's and Firemen's Union and represents Middlesbrough in Parliament. His independence in politica; matters is shown by the fact that he was the only opponent of the introduction of Mr. Churchill's Labour Exchanges Bill in the House of Commons on Thursday.

* * I do not know what Moses would have done if he had had to face such financial problems, but I am certain that Mr. Lloyd- George is leading us out of a very cruel Egypt.—Mr. Will Drooks, M.P. Good motherhood; manly. fatherhood; happy, healthy childhood—with these three qualities, and afterwards with education, training, discipline and character, there is no reason for us to fear for the future of the State.—Mr. John Burns, M.P. We have advanced to such a stage, at present, that we see a German soldier in every waiter; we look up into the skies to see airships; and we almost dread to take in the post in the morning for fear of being greeted with a Gorman bomb.—Lord Weardele. * * *

At its last meeting the Town Council decided to open the whole of Victoria Park to' the public and yesterday afternoon the members of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee visited the Park to determine what steps should be taken to carry out the scheme. It was agreed to remove all the fences so as to convert the land into one huge park, and also to construct a road round the park, following the bend of the river and returning to the entrance gates. This will provide a pleasant walk nearly two miles in length and we have no doubt that it will be appreciated by the townspeople. We hear it is the intention of the Committee to allow the park to be used for foot- hair and cricket to a greater extent than has hitherto been practicable. There is just one sad reflection in considering these public improvements. The opening out of the Park was the pet scheme of the late Alderman Ellison, and at the last Council meeting he expressed his satisfaction that after 12 years labour the park was to be dedicated fully to the public use. He had, at least, the satisfaction of knowing that before he laid down ,his life's work, part of it had come to fruition.

* The local tariff reformers are not to have things all their own way, for the Progressive Labour League has just inaugurated an open air campaign in support of Free Trade. The opening meeting was most encouraging, for the attendance was large and the speeches were followed with keen interest and enthusiasm. Mr. Joseph Howes, who is such a favourite with Warrington audiences,was in his best form, and in a lucid and telling style he pointed out that it is not Free Trade but the toll levied on industry by the landlords which handicaps us in the commercial struggle. We would commend to our readers his striking illustration of the injustice of the present system of mining royalties. During the passage to New York and back the great ship " Lusitania" con- sumes 16,800 tons of coal on which a royalty of £1,050 is paid to the landlord, but the wages paid to the 333 men. who work in the stokehole amount to only £538 103. It is by tackling such anomalies as this and not by taxing the necessaries of life that the nation as a whole will be benefited, and pro- gressives hail with satisfaction the policy of land reform which the Government have boldly inaugurated in their democratic Budget.

Death of the Rev. -Frank Lake Good.

Many of our readers will hear with regret that the death took place on Wednesday of the Rev. Frank Lake Good at the Royal Southern Hospital, after undergoing an operation for the amputation of his foot on Friday last. He seemed to be recovering and was gaining strength wonderfully, when on Tues- day all hopes of restoration to health were abandoned as the result of a relapse and on Wednesday at a late hour he passed away. The deceased gentleman had been suffering from gangrene of the foot since Christmas, but only on the Bth inst. was it thought necessary to call a doctor in. The inedical gentleman at once ordered him to the Hospital at Liverpool. The rev, gentleman began his ministry in 1881, when he worked as layman to the Rev. Burman Cassin, M.A., at St. George the Martyr Southwark. He did some very good work in that district, and the many friends he , made there were very sorry to lose him when he came to Warrington hi 1887. Here he ministered to the people of the Emmanuel Church until 1891, when on giving up ministerial work he was presented with an illuminated address. Since then he has preached occasionally, but on the whole he lived a. quiet life. The funeral took place yesterday after- noon at the Warrington Cemetery. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton presided. The chief mourners were : Messrs. J. Hindley (brother-in-law). E. W. Whitehouse, H. and J. Mottram, H. Hindley, W. Freeman (nephews), Amongst others present were the Rev. Frank Vaughan (pastor of Emmanuel Church) and Messrs. E. and R. Lunt. The deceased gentleman leaves a widow. Messrs. Johnson were the undertakers.

Little Boy Drowned. There was a sad drowning fatality in Howley Fields on Tuesday evening when Edward Graham, the eight years old son of Mr. and Mrs. Graitam, of 23, General- street, met with death while playing with other boys. The deceased was trying to reach a cork floating on a pit neat the houses when he fell in and was drowned. At the inquest last evening, Coroner Brighouse remarked on the danger of such a pit being unfenced when only 1:5 yards from the highway. Mr. F. G. Taylor (who represented the Firth Company Limited, who owned the pit), said the pit was formerly used as a clay pit by his clients and at that it was fenced round, but the fencing had been con- tinually taken away, so it was decided to abandon the pit. Since it was abondoned the firm had endeavoured to get the pit filled up.—The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death" and added that the pit was not sufficiently fenced. A fair number- of members turned out for the run to Chester on Saturday last, despite the distance and the unsettled weather. With the exception of one halt the outward journey was accomplished by good riding in respectlble time. Chester reached, interest was again renewed in many attractions which the city offers, and tea provided there by ex-captain Delmage received due justice. The return ride was made at a decent pace, and Warrington reached in the evening after a most enjoyable outing. There was a record attendance this season of Bowlers at Sankey on Wednesday, with a beautiful wirming, and turf in the best condition,the woods were kept rolling until dusk. —The midweek run to Preston Brook and Little Leigh on Thursday was well attended,and the evening being pleasant, and the roads in good condition, members enjoyed a glorious spin. On Tuesday Mr. and Mrs. Benjamin Bennett, of 15, Victoria-road, Stockton Heath, celebrated their golden wedding. This interesting event took place at the residence of their son, Mr. Thomas Bennett, of Clap Gates Farm, Bewsey,. amid many manifestations of rejoicing. The reception lasted from 3 to 8.30, and- the old farm house was tested to its utmost capacity to find accommodation for the family gather- ing. Mr. and Mrs. Bennett have been blessed with a family of ten children, every member being present .to do them honour on this auspicious oecr!sion. The function was cele- brated in a quiet manner, no invitations having been given outside the family circle. The whole of the family present were: —Mr. and Mrs. Baxter Bennett, Sankey ; Mr. and Mrs. Edwin Plinston, Wildei spool Causeway; Mr. and Mrs. Ernest Bennett, Latchford ; Mr. and Mrs. 11. C. Jones, Wilderspool Causeway ; Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Bennett; Mr. and Mts. John Lowe, Golborne ; Mr. and Mrs. Ben Bennett, Froghall-lane ; Mr. and Mrs. . Innocent, Holt ; Mr. and Mrs. J. D. Smithy, Brighton; and Miss Mary Bennett. The honoured coupe were the recipients of numerous lettets and telegrams during the day from friends and relations. It is interesting to note that, although Mr. Bennett is in his eightieth year, he is still hale and hearty and a most vigorous walker. He still converses with clearness, of events which happened in the forties, au if they were but the ordinary happenings of yesterday. Mr. and Mrs. Bennett were married at Manchester Cathedral on the 18th May, 1859. LAWN MOWERS at store prices for cash. Repairs by special plant.—J. S. GARLAND, Ironmonger Bridge st.—Advt.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE. MARRIED WOMEN- TEACHERS. The Question of Exemptions.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee WAS held on Monday evening under the presidency of Councillor J. Fulton-Smith. There were also present: Mr. H. Roberts (deputy-chairman), Alderman Bennett, Councillors Baitun, Dickenson, Lord, Singleton, Revs. James Davies, C3llOll Stevenson, Messrs. C. Sawkins Burgess, T. P. Bradshaw, R. T. Fa'rclough, A. J. Jolley, u• E. Parker, E. Plinston, C. H. Unsworth, J. Upson and G. Waiter; J. Moore Murray (director), R. B. Roberts (assistant director). John Fairhurst (treasurer).

THE LATE ALDERMAN ELLISON. A resolution expressing regret at the death of Alder- man Ellison, and condoling with the widow and family was passed on the proposition of the chairman, all the members standing,.

MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS. SOME EXEMP rioNs. The School Management Committea considered com- munications received in respect of certain married women teachers affected by the Committee's regulations. After careful consideration of these communications it was resolved that the reasons submitted by or on behalf of Mrs. Mary Dooley, Mrs. Mary Gittoes, and Mrs. Emil' Constance Scarisbrick be accepted as special reasons within the meaning of regulation 3of the regulations in question and that their services be continud. It was resolved that threa months notice from tha 31st day of March 1909, be given to Mrs. Langeon Isabel Cann to terminate her engagement as Superi .reed -'it of Cooliery Instruction on the 31st Angus . 19.19.

Th ouni t eaccepted Mrs. Elizabeth Hankinson's resig2 t on of her position as a certificated assistant mist ,; in the senior mixed department of the St. AnrCs • hool, aid it was resolved that resolution No. C 2471 resc.nded ; and that the notice given therein to Al s :-;lizaheth Hankinson to terminate her engage- men. a a cer:Micsted assistant mistress in the senior naixe ..epart ment of the St. Ann's school be withdrawn. Th • 1: -v. Canon STEVENSON, in moving the confirms- Lion o die minutes of the School Managament Corn- mittev, mlliidLd to the above question. The committee woule, he said, see that the circumstances of certain marrie I lady teachers in the town had been considered, and it had been felt that it was right they should retilin their position in three instances, but not in four. Speaking personally as one who voted in the minority against the whole question, he could only say that he regretted the decision of the committee. But it was no use going back, and he rejoiced that, at all events, three of the ladies had been retained.

...... Mr. OrsoN-seconded the motion for the passing of the minutes and atter some discussion it was carried.

}AIRFIELD SCHOOL DEADLOCK.

At a meeting of. the School Management Committee a letter was read from the Managers of the Fairfield school (in reply to the letter sent to them in accord- ance with the Committee's decision at their meeting on the Ist April, 1909), from which it appearel that the Managers were not preplrel to submit for theCona- mittee's consideration any proposals for the orgfintzatioft of that school which • might lead to the ultimate amalg motion of the two Girls' departments. Linder these circumstances, the Director was instructed to inform the Board of Education that the Committee were of opinion that no good purpose would be served by a Mahr ce.nference between the Committee and the Managers of this school, and to request the Board to take such action as will bring about the desired amalgamation of the departments in question at as early a dr.te as possible. GRAMMAR SCHOOL SCHOLARSHIPS. Mr. WALKER asked if it was a fict that. there were to be no examinations this year for scholarships in the Grammar School. He understood several parents had incurred considerable expense with a view to the examination and now there was a possibility of dis- appointment.

*Air. BURGESS: Allow me, as a representative of the Grammar School, t 3 explain that the arrangement between the Grammar School and this Committee is that 16 Boteler scholarships shall he maintained, allow- ing this Committee certain scholarships which would bring the number up to 20 per cent, of the whole of the pupils of the school. At the present moment there are six Council scholarships already given by the Grammar School to boys belcnging to this authority, so that altogether we have now 22 free scholars in the school, and the total number of pupils is less than 110. There- fore there arc more than 20 per cent, of the number of pupils who have free scholaaships, and on that account there can be no further scholarships offered to the authority this year. If in the order of things any of the Council scholarships fall out the governors of the Grammar School will give to this authority scholarships in their place. _ _

Mr. UPSON said the p3int was this, that it had been known in the town for a number of years that examina- tions were held for fres scholarships at the Grammar School, and there was a general understanding that the examination this year would not be held. Were they to understand that t!:e numbers of free scholarships were not t) be so many Boteler scholarships and sa many Borough scholarshies but rather that the total number was to be limited to 20 per cent, of the number of boys in the school ? He could see, if the governors had a specific number of scholarships and they as a committee bad a certain 'minter, that the two at one time might be greater than 20 per cent, of the total number. Were they to understand that the govern Ors were limiting free scholars to 2) per cent. of the number of boys in, the school or that they would give their 16 scholarships plus a specific number of scholar- ships for the Education Committee? Mr. BURGESS said the agreement was that the governors should maintain 16 Boteler scholarships and that other scholarships should be offered to the town so as to make up the number of free scholarships to 20 per cent. of the whole school. There were two Boteler tcholarships to be offered this year because there would be two vacancies at the end of the term. THE ACCOMMODATION QUESTION. Mr. PrJssToN, in proposing the adoption of the minutes of the School" Attendance Committee, called atteation to the following minute :—"A communication was submitted from the Board of Education, forwarding a schedule showing the re-assessed accommodation of the public elementary schools in the borough, but con- sideration thereof was deferred until the views of the several bodies of minagers upon the suggested reduc- tions in the accommodation are received." He said the committee were waiting until the managers of the different schools had sent to them their information. While dealing with that, it, was time they should remember the position exactly they would be placed in, if the Board of Ethic ition insistei, as they were likely to do, upon•the terms of the communication they sent. It meant that the school accommodation in Warrington, which was vastly short at the present time, would be in a very much more serious position. The total accom- modatioa of the schools would Le 12,903, and the average number on the books for the year ending March 1909 was 13,631, a deficiency of 728. The present accommodation was 14,710, and the accommodation according to the proposals of the Board of Education, was to be 12,903—a reduction of 1807.. This did not include Trinity infant school which had been con- demned, and where there were 274 scholars. Provision was being made for a school for 1,260 children, so that that would leave a deficiency of 781. This was a very important matter and one they were bound to consider, or else they vuld find themselves in an awkward position. Mr. BrtAnsftaev seconded and the minutes were passed unanimously.

WARRINGTON COUNTY PETTY SESSIONS.

Motorists Caught in Police" Trap," At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday before Messrs. Charles White (in the chair) C. E. Parker, P. Forshaw, and Roger C. Parr, ricrac° Augustus King of Garton was summoned for driving a motor car on the 18th Apr:l at the rate of 30 miles an hour along Warrington-road, Penketh.—Mr. 11. J. West- brook (Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.) appeared for defendant, who was not present, and pleaded not guilty. —Sergeant Smith said that at a pre-arranged signal from P.C. Corlett he timed defendant's motor car along ame :sured distance of a quarter of a mile. The time occupied was just 30 seconds, ELIA it wolkaci out at 30 miles an bour.—Examined by Mr. Westbrook he said there was nothing to obstruct the signal of P.C.Corlett. lie had no difficulty in seeing the signal. There might be half a dozen people in the road. - He saw a speedo- meter on tire car, but he did not examine it.—P.C. Corlett said that he was on duty in plain clothes and as the front of the car reached him he gave the signal to Sergeant Smith. He had never seen a motor car travelling as fast as that one through Penketh before.—Examined by Tr. Vestbrook he said there was a bead in the road, but still the signal could be seen very plainly.—P.C. Houghton said the car was going towards Liverpool. He stopped the car CO or 70 yards past the "trap," and lolked at the speel indicator but it was at zero.—Reginald R.King,who was in the car at the timesaid th tt when the ear was steppe,' the officer said that the car had travelled a quarter of a mile in 30 seconds.
