SHIP CANAL ACCIDENT.

Dispute About Compensation Award.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, made an applicatiora on behalf of John Bore, of Poacher's Lane, off Thelicall Lane, Latchford, for the registration of a memorandum of agreement under the Workmen's Compensation Act whereby the Man- chester Ship Canal Company agreed to pay him 12s. 3d., per week.—Mr. A. Newman (Messrs. Grundy, Kershaw and Co.. solicitors,Manchester)represented the compa fly. Mr. Browne stated that !lore was a gatemen at Latchford Locks and on December 12th last he was en duty when the steamship Kathleen of Glasgow entered the leek. Ho was making fast the rope to a capstan when it suddenly tightened and he was thrown into the canal which was very deep at this point. He was crushed between the vessel and the etty wall and sustained injuries which necessitated his being teken to the Infirmary where he remained for 21 days. As a result of the accident, applicant bad now a great dread of water and he was also subject to faint- ing fits, so that it would be dangerous for him to work at the locks. The company paid him compensation at the rate of 12s. 3d. per week. Correspondence took place and applicant offered to try and do some light. work. On April 20th, the superintendent of the com- pany at Manchester wrote stating that the company had no objectien to the registration of a memorandum of agreement for payment of 12s. 3d. per week. As regarded finding light employment, they had no work in the neighboerhood of Latchford away from the water so that for the present they would hive to continuo to pay him compensation and if in future his dread of water and fainting fits left him they would do their best to find. him more suitaide employment. When the agreement wee drawn up, however, the company disputed it and alleged that Hore was able to work, thus entirely changing their tone in the course of about ten days. Bore had made several attempts to work but found he was quite incapable of doing any. He could not be malingering. for it would he to his advantage to work and receive 245. 6d. instead of 123. 3d. per week. Mr. Newman denied that the Company had gone hack on their offer. The agreement as drawn up preclude] them from applying for a reduction of the compenla- tion. He suggested that as there would be a conflict of medical testimony in the case it was a euitable.one for reference to a medical referee who could receive the assistance of one doctor repre- senting eech side. After conferen-e between the solicitors, Mr. Newman agreed to the registration of the agreement with the omission of a statement ta which the company took exception that Hore unlit and will be for some unascertainable period to resume normal employment." His Honeur accordingly registered the agreement which set. forth that three of Here's left ribs were dislocated, that his lungs were punctured or badly bruised, which still caused spitting of blood from time to time. His right shoulder was also injured and he was subject to fainting fits. The company agreed to continue the compensation as long as applicant was incapacitated.

Mr. Newman now made application for a review of the award on the ground that incapacity had ceasecl. He called

Dr. Jagn,nflittchford,who stated that he had attended Hore first at his own request and then on behalf of the company. He first saw him on March 21st when be complained of pains in the right shoulder and occasional fainting. Witnesa, however, could not find anything which would account for pains. He had evera 1 other interviews with the man but in none of his examinations could he find any physical cause for pain. On April Ist, with Hore's conent-be put him unler choloroforrn. He bad complained that be could not raise his right arm %ithout pain but while he was under choloroform witness work,d the arm about and all the movements of the arm and shoulder were quite free. There was no physical obstruction or infirmity discoverahle. On April 30th TA itness put Hoye through several tests at the request of the Canal Company and found that he was aide to raise his arms niturally.' Leaving out the question of shock, which it was not, possible for him to estimate, he considered that physi- cally speaking the man was fit to follow any employ- ment.

By Mr. Browne : The ends of two ribs gave evidence of having been displaced, and there was slight swelling. He denied telling Mrs. Hore that something in the shoulder had been out of place, or that his nerves were complet-ly wreck-A. There was no irregularity in the heart's action. If he was eubject to fainting fits, it would be unsafe for him to work at the locks. Dr. McCune, one of the house sorgeons at the War- rington Infirmary, who had been subpoenaed for the respondent, was called by the comp iny. He stated that Hort) was in the Infitmary from December 12th to January 6th. When admitted he was suffering from dis- location of the outer end of the collar bule on the right side. The breast hone was depressed and three of the ribs on the left side were displaced forwar.l. He was spitting blood copiously. Witness soundel the chest and clme to the conclusion tint there was some tearing of the surfaces of b )th lungs. lie was suffering from severe shook and collapse. Witness saw him frequently whilst be was- in the institution and had also examined him on May 3rd and llth. He was of opiniou. that !lore was at present unfit for any kind of work. He had some persistence of the shock which he received at the time of the accident and was in a very nervous condition. The action of the heart had increased and there was some restriction of the movement of his right arm. On May 3rd he appeand to be able to do light work, hut after be had worked for a week the increased action of the heart became manifest.

By Mr. Browne: It would be dangerous for Here to he employei at Latchford locks in his present condi- tion. and he ought not to attempt even light work. Mr. Newman said that in view of Dr. McCune's evidence, there could be no conclusion but that the man at present was entitled ta his full compensation. The company must take their own course with reference to a NI t her application to diminish or extinguish the awari, but at present he could nt,t go behind an independent witness's testimony. He therefore offend no objection to the order and the company would pay compensation until such time as they were advised it should te Bt3pped. His Honour accordingly orJered the agreement to stand ani awarded qualifying fees to Drs. Richmond, McCune and Johnstone.

AN _EXCITING INCIDENT AT &CATTLE SALE

A few days ago' says" Farm and Horne, there was a joint sale held by three farmers at the quiet little village of Sherborne, in Gloucestershire. They were good cattle and sold well, one of them getting £350 more for his portion than he had dared to hope for. A cow came in the ring, tracing her descent to the old Mason blood through Pye, the ancestress of a capital tribe of that name, possessed by the numerous Game family, so many of whom had Shorthorn herds. The owner was desirous that one of his cows should fetch "three figures," and after a great effort on the part of the auctioneer this was effected,among loud cheering from sympathetic neighbours. But they little knew— what was coming next. The little bull calf in the ring with its dam was barely forty-eight hours old, and very weak on its legs. Of course, the buyer of the cow wanted the calf to go with it, but, as luck would have it, a breeder present also had a strong fancy for the youngster. The result was an obstinate fight between two bidders for posses- sion, and, amid a scene of frantic excitement, the little animal was at last parted from It 3 dam, the buyer having to pay 130 guineas for it.

vasey-road Wesley Guild.

Methodist Free Church, Buckley-street. The Sunday school anniversary services werepreuched on Sunday /ftst in the above place of worship, by Mr. Pinles, of Chester, this being the occasion of his 16th anniversary. He preached at the morning service fram the text he took on his first visit to Buckley-street Church, Song of Solomon ii., 11 and 12.—1 n the after- noon Mr. J. Bate occupied the chair, and Mr. Pinches gave an address to the children, parents and teachers, and based his remarks on the dut'e3 and responsibility of parents in following up in the home the good lessons and instructions given by the Sunday school te tellers. —Nlise Eliza Scott (age eight) rendered very beautifully the s ,lo " dreamt I was in heaven."—At the evening service every available seat in the Church was occup!ed, Mr. Pinches again being in the pulpit. He preached from the text Gen. 41-57, and in his dis2ourse illustrated the life of Jozeph as being typical of the life of Christ. Taylor, who must have exercised great patience with the train:llg of the children to have brought them to such perfection with their singing. Wycliffe P.S.A. •

There was a good attend tnce at an open meeting of this society on Sunday. Alderman D. Tinnion, J.P., presided and spoke on " ihe influence we have upon each other." He pointed out that everyone had influ- ence although some ima:ined that, they had not. Yet in all, there was an influence ei her for good or evil. It was in the province of all to exert a good influence. He hoped all present would try to create a &rod impression upon othets.—Mr. Tom Harrison, of Oldham, was the speaker. The subject of his remarks was, "The stone was rolled away." He said he bad been trying to roll away the stones from the lives of men and women this last 30 years, and gave one or two- illustrations of men and women who were once the victims of sin but now were trefel men and women for Christ,in whose cases he ha d bad the honour of rolling away the stone from their lives.He referred to the evils of drinking and garahling.There was plenty to do in Warrington. plenty of stenos to roll away,-and ha hoped the young men present would from now enter upon this rood work of winning nien for Christ.—Miss Alice Bridge sang sweetly and effectively "A Dream of Paradise," ".God that made earth and heaven." Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Duch. Sunday Sr.liol Anniversary. Last year the anniversmry of the above Sunday school was not what may be termed on the whole " a distinct succeis." However this year it may be safely said that they were, as was evidenced by the large con- gregations that assembled on Sunday in the Church in the morning and in the Parr Hall afternoon and even- ing. The collect'ons while not as large as in some previous years amounted to a little over .£3O, a proof of the fact that the excellent work of the school for the rising generation is appreciated by the townspeople. In the morning the Rev. A. H. Walker, B A., of Liverp )01, was the preacher, and he did as many others have done lately—he gave a sermon which dealt with thp influence of people on children. lie said that the conduct of all should be such as was worthy of imitaticn, for by the conduct of people the child fashioned his ideas and habits. _

_ In the afternoon the service ens held in the Parr Hall so as to he able to accommodate the large number of people who were expectcd--as in former years—to turn' up to hear the singing of the children, the solo of Miss Siloner, who sang "The Promise of Life," and Mr. John Lipson's address. Mr. R. 11. Meldrum occupied the chair. The singing of the children was very good, the hynms being peculiarly adapted to them. Mr. C. R. Muir was the conductor, Mr. A.Trow the orgauist, uni Mr. Drough assisted at the piano. Mr. JOHN UPSON in a very plactical speech said that great, good and noble men at set periods of the world's Listory who were dissatisfied with the organisation of human society, disgusted with its chaos and confusion, its social injustice, its economic wrong, had put their loagings into forecasts of what human society should be were selfishness eliminated from human hearts. Thus we had Piato's "Republic," Sir Thomas Moore's' "Utopia," and in our own day those wondes ful creations of William Morris and H. G. Wells which had tried to depict under what conditions we should live were those human failings which completely marred our rresent organized society removed. Beautiful as those creations were, stimulating as they were to thought, and we believe to some extent provo- ative of reform, yet in simplicity,grandeur, realisability they did not reach the heights of some of the forecasts, and visions of the prophets of olden times. For instances, Zechariah having seen a wasted country side, tsrinny, oppression and despsir imagined a state—an ideal state—which he called the New Jerusalem. He said : "In the New Jerusalem we shall find these things. Old men and old women shall dwell in the'streets at Jerusalem . . . and the streets of the city shall be full of boys and girls playing in the streets thereof." That old prophet by inference would Iced us to understand that old age and childhood were two of the greatest blessings we could have, that their welfare should always bethe fist to be considered, end their interests always the first to be promoted and cherished. That was exceedingly strange. in the first place old people and children were helpless, none productive, and a burden upon energy and the resources of others. It was sometimes said that a man was only valuable to the State in so far as he was a productive agent. That suegested that old men and women, boys and girls were of no value at all. Recent legislation seemed dimly to have grasped the truth that that old prophet saw. In the Government's Childrees' Act and its provision for old age the Government seemed to have got hold of the truths which all the churches had seen wish clear eyes for many years past. We could understand the helplessness and the non-productive- ness of those he had mentioned,aud yet how we grumbled at the Education rate, and how we squirmed at the taxes that the Budget imposed upon us for the maintenance of old men and women. Continuing the speaker said the citizens of the State were not made noble by what they tried to do for themselves. They were not made noble by their efforts for social advant- ages.. They were not made men and women in the true sense, as they strived to get positions in the world. Our citizens were made noble by what they did for those who could do nothing in return. The teachers had laboured during the past year in that very successful Sanday scho and had done much for the children, but by so doing they did more good for themselves. By their labour in the Sunday School they had rendered service to the State which could never be estimated, and they had built some of the walls of the -New Jerusalem. The future of the State depended upon the child and as the child was to-day tbo State would bolo the next genera- tion. He was speaking in a county where much child labour was exploited, but he was thankful to say there was not so much of it in this town. A little time back he and the Director of Education placed the boy of - his school who worked in the evenings field on Saturdays on one side, an-I those who did not work on tho other In every case the child who did not work, but played in the streets, was taller and heavier. He msintained that there was to-day sally too much of the exploitation of child labour. Concluding Mr. Upson said that a State was judged by the way it treated its children. If the children were despised the State was accursed. In the New Jerusalem our children would be free and happy, let us do all we could to Snake this town a city of our God. - -

The evening service was conducted by the Rev. A. WALKER, who made a special appeal to the csthgregation to surrender themselves to God. He took as his text "I have seen God face to face and my life is preserved" (Gen. xxxii. pt. v. SO). What the rev, gentleman feared was that the country should turn to inOdelity. " You may go on building your Dreadnoughts," he went on, "out the Dreadnoughts will never save. The future is in the hands of the nations :vho look to God. It is not the yellow peril that I am frightened about, it is the peril of unbelief; it is the peril of lack of thoughtful reverential piety. We want Christ in our home life and Church life." He. asked his audience to " give in" to Got Certainly it would he a defeat, but out of itwould come a great victory. . _ _ _

During the service Miss Silcock- sang "0 Divine Redeemer," and Miss llew itt Cook the solo part in one of the hymns.

EDUCATIONAL SYSTEM CRITICISED.

- At the mect'n7, of tge Chinhira County Education Committee, on Monday at Crowe, Dr. Hodgson presid- ing, Dr. Charlt s Wilson moved a resolution asking for a report as to the alleged partial failure of the present educa ional system, and contended the present system was on the wrong lines, and suggested it was a pal tial failure. The great. mi,take, he said, was that to-day boy was being treated as a ininiature man, and thtt the teacher did too much for the Loy and the boy did not do enough for himself. The Chairman said there was never a time when the educational system was so critic:sod, and there was always a disposition to say that the education of the present generation w is not so good as that of the previous generation. The whole attitude was one of considerable Mg ui ry.—Mr. T. Raffles Bulley (Liscard) agreed with much that had been said, and suggested that it should be brought before the. Curriculum Coinmittee.—After further discussion Dr. Wilson withdrew his resolution. On the motion of the Chairman, Dr. Wilson was elected -a member of the Elementary Education Committee.

Mr. Will Crooks, M.P. has arranged to leave England in September next for a tour around the world. The trip has been recommended by his medical adviser to provide change and rest necessary to re-establish him iu health,

An official return issued on Wednesday shows that during 1908 there were 22 sentences of corporal punish- ment. The ages of those receiving punishment ranged from 17 to 48, and the number of strokes from 6 to 18.

A SUMMER PILGRIMAGE. 0 Ne'dle•W\r--"N -r\e'A./ 0

• BOOTHBAIIK. ' Pi OLD HOUSE OF MANY MEMORIES

As Rostherne is on the visiting list of many readers just now we purpose to direct them to an old homestead which lies on their way thither, and which will trans- port them for: the moment to another world and other memories. The world is. the Methodist world, it is true, and it is not everyone whiPwill be keenly interested, but the chief figures in our story were makers of history and this may be claimed as. a suffi- cient reason for introducing them. Travelling through Lymm we leave the main road to the right at Agden. A country lane kirts the park of Agden Hall, and after a turn to the left we reach a quiet farmhouse in front of which a tall oak tree stands like a giant sentinel. This is "John Wesley's Oak" and we have come to look at the house in which he and Whitefield and a host of others came to preach in the stirring days of a century and a half ago. Half a mile away is the Manchester and Chester Road, tho Watling Street of the, Romans, and motor cars are rushing over it amid blinding clouds of dust. But quiet reigns supreme in the secluded road which we are exploring, and but for the distant hooting of the cars we may imagine ourselves back in the 18th century.

St," into the tiriy lane, and may reflect Upon the influence thek were destined to have upon each other's lives. The worthy but fickle Grace Murray came between them, and by marrying John Bennett deprived Wesley of a famous helper and placed his life under a deep and sombre shadow. Wesley preached twice at Boothbank and spent the night there. He makes no detailed record in his journal but a zealous local scribe preserved a. record of his texts. The first reference in his Journal is under date May Bth, 1747. "In the evening 1 preached at Boothbank among a quietand loving people." On three other occasions he records visits, August 29th, 1749; October 10th, 1749 ; and March 31st, 1753.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
BOOTHBANK.

The old house is of the Elizabethan architecture and has .probably been in use through three centuries. Our.interest begins in 1744, in which year it became the home of John and Alice Cross, a newly-married pair

===== 0002601_19090522_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
BOOTHBANK. AND "JOHN WESLEY'S OAK."

The timbered house in the centre wo

who were settling to farmk life. Alice Cross had met John Wesley when she was a servant maid and had been deeply impressed by his conversation. In 1738, the year from which he dated the birth of his own religious zeal, he visited Manchester, calling at Altrincham, Holmes Chapel and Knutsford. -Methodism as we understand it was not yet born, and field preaching was far from his thoughts, but he preached most effec- tively .at dinner tables, being determined, as he wrote in his journal, "to lose no oppor- tunity of awakening, instructing or exhorting any whom we might meet with in our journey:.

- It would be of interest to Wesley iv after years to learn how surprisingly and to what extent his wayside sowing had borne fruit. One man who listened forthwith opened his house for preaching at Sinderland Green. Alice Cross also established a preaching centre, and thus it came about that before the country at large had heard of the Methodist revival, there were cottages in these remote highways licensed for preach- ing. John Cross did not share the enthu- siasm of his young wife and was not willing to open their home for preaching. But it was hard to hold out against the strong will and stronger appeal of Alice. "John Cross" she is reported to have said "wilt thou go to heaven with me, for I am determined not to go to hell with thee." And John yielded! Though almost masculine in energy and determination, Alice Cross had the gentleness of a true saint, and some very happy pen- pictures were drawn by those who knew her. Thus John Pawson, a man famous among Methodist preachers, wrote :—" She was one of the most zealous, active and spiritually minded women I ever knew. I had heard a good deal about Alice, and when I went to the house she was standing in the doorway. She was dressed exceedingly plain, but remarkably clean, and if I cap form any just idea how a person would look who had just come from a world of happy spirits, I should suppose that she very nearly resembled guch a one ; and I seriously think, more so than any woman I ever yet saw in the whole course of my life." With a husband willing to co-operate she quickly made her home a famous religious centre. The large kitchen of the farm house made a fine auditorium, and she herself preached and exhorted at the Sunday services. Long before Lancashire or Cheshire 14 a Methodist Chapel the old house was licensed for preaching. Its strong-minded matron wrought cease- lessly for "the spread of scriptural holiness" and the timbered walls of her house became the asylum of persecuted or distressed Methodists from far and near. Her warn- ings were given without reservation, and on one occasion she stopped the Cheshire Hunt as it passed down the lane and warned the hunters to" flee from the wrath to come." Lord Stamford and Sir Harry Mainwaring were among the hunters and" listened to her warning before riding on."

Ls the home of John and Alice Cross

[II. E. TusuL

pany. We visited the Societies in Rochdale, Manchester and Ho'me in Lancashire, and Boothhank in Cheshire." These associations would be sufficient in themselves to make the homestead famous, but it has a distinctive Methodist fame as the early centre of the organised Methodism of Cheshire and Lancashire. On April 20th, 1752,the old house was the scene of a meeting of more than common interest, the mentfon of which must not be omitted. On that day the Churches of the district were organised and the first " Quarter day " in Cheshire was held. The account book of the meeting is preserved, and the first page is declared by Competent judges to be in the handwriting of John Wesley himself. The Churches represented were Chester, Bolton, Man- chester, Davyhulme, and eight village meet- ings scattered through Cheshire. It says much for the influence of BoOthbank that it should be chosen as the centre for this first meeting, and the occasion assumes uncommon interest and importance when we remember that from it the Chester, Manchester, War- rington, Northwich, Bolton, Altrincham, and several other Wesleyan Methodist circuits have sprung. The history of Boothbank is a story of quiet usefulness. That it was a famed centre of early Methodism is proved by the notable preachers who were attracted to it, the mere mention of whose labours would more than absorb our present space.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN WESLEY AND BOOTHBANK.

The first efforts ' at organization in Methodism were made about 1745,. and in that year John Bennett was placed in charge of a round " of churches in the North of England. His " round " took him to White- haven and compassed Derbyshire and York- shire—a circuit surely big enough for any main's ambition. It was in company with Bdtinett that John Wesley first visited Boothbank. We may picture them on horseback as they turn from " Watling

===== 0002601_19090522_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
BOOTHBANK RELICS.

Several left behind them at the old house their marks and memorials. John Wesley wrote his name on the window pane of the prophet's chamber. Other markings, - also written with a diamond, are :—" Faith with- out works is dead. Ed. Pyke." "GO on to Perfection." " Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart. November the 12th, 1766, J. Allen." Joseph Guildford preached here 1764. God is Love." John Nelson,who began to visit Boothbank in 1746, made for John Cross a wooden boot jack, which is carefully kept by Mr. D. Cross, of Timperley. The movable pulpit has gone, and so has the pfeacher's teapot, but the corner cupboard and inscribed window panes are preserved. For nearly ninety years the services were held in the kitchen of the farm, and then in 1834 the present Boothbank Chapel was built to continue the work and maintain the traditions of the older preaching place. Alice Cross died in 1774, aged 65. John sur- vived her 21 years. Their resting place is mazked by a flat stone near the south wall of Rostherne Church. For three generations the Cross family inhabited the old house, and it remains scarcely changed since the days when it first received the Methodist preachers. Externally, however, there was an attempt at "restoration "-last year, the thatched roof (shades of John Ruskin) being covered with corrugated iron. The prophet's chamber is a small room upon the ground floor, and save for the removal of the inscribed window panes is unchanged. The large kitchen, too with its broad leaded window, is unaltered, and we can still inspect the stable in which Wesley and Whitefield, and countless numbers of their followers stabled their horses.

After reflecting upon the 'ban' of Rostherne, the traveller may find it interest- ing to reflect upon the men who faced so kravely the ban ecclesiastical,' and who in promoting a revival of religion incidentally did so much to • raise the English people towards social freedom and enlightenment. A. M.

GEORGE WIIITEFIELD.

The visit mentioned under date October 19th, 1749, has unusual interest, for Wesley was accompanied on this occasion by -George Whitefield. They had travelled through Yorkshire, arousing unusual storms of feel- ing and opposition, and came together to the welcome quiet of Cheshire lanes. -Wesley's journal, while it mentions their joint labours in Yorkshire, does not say that Whitefield was his companion through Cheshire, but a letter"written by Whitefield to Lady Fanny Shirley on October 25th, describes the tour. The !`prophet's chamber"at Boothbank would therefore shelter both these intrepid evangelists on the night of October 19th, 1749, and it is known for certain that both preached under the branches of the oak tree before they passed upon their eventful ways. George Whitefield was again at Boothbank in October, 1753, and preached more than once at John and Alice Cross's. A letter which is extant shows that another famous friend of Wesley preached at Boothbank. This was William Grimshaw, vicar of Haworth, who says in a letter to Charles Wesley, on August 20th, 1747: "'Last week I struck out into Lancashire and Cheshire, Mr. Bennett bearing me corn-

MR. JOSEPH CLARE.

I was born in the Old Road, Latchford, on January 14th, 1830, the son of a shoemaker.' The families of both my father and mother belonged to Cheshire. I was familiar from my earliest days with the keenest poverty. My father's income was about 15s. per week, 2s. of which was in demand for house rent. My mother was a factory hand and went at six each morning, rising at twelfe o'clock on Sunday night: to •do her washing before going to the mill t six on Monday morning. As •I . was the eldest child it fell to my duty to attend to the housewoitk, and I performed in addition the duties of cook. Not that the cooking required any special skill, for our breakfat consisted of ,porridge and bread, our dinner of potatoes and bacon and our tea of bread and treacle. Nine chil- dren were born to my father and Mother and for this family a quarter of a pound of bacon sufficed , for dinner. For Sunday, however, half a pound was purchased. 'and we had double allowance. Though our fare was 'scanty we enjoyed the best of health and for 20 years I do not remember a doctor entering our house. It may interest my readers to know that seven of the family survive, the youngest being 65. Our ages total to more than 500 years and the average age is 74, not a bad record for a family reared upon simp!O fare. My father had theories Of his own about the up-bringing of children.. He wanted them to grow to large stature and as a means of attaining this end would not allow us to go to either school or work. He had his reward in seeing every one of his children exceed his own stature. My early days were taken up in bathing and games and the earning of an occasional penny' for holding horses. When nine or ten years old I Went a little to the National School. I well remember at this period the great flood. The water from the rivei. had reached Reynolds' Tannery as I went to school and had to be waded through. By the time school was over the streets were impassable and People plied the street in boats. My real schooling, however, was obtained at a later date and was an indirect outcome Of the greatest misfortune of my life. In 1850, when I had attained the.age of twenty years, a serious affection of the knee of two years standing necessitated the amputation of my leg. I had heard of chloroform, and told Dr. Hardy the surgeon that I must have sonic for the operation.He demurred and warned me of the danger, but I insisted and for two hours was under the influence of it whilst my leg was removed. This operation gave me .the distinction of being the first man in my native town to ba placed under the sedative. For 12 months I lay in bed and my sufferings were increased by the knowledge that bad trade was bringing my parents almost to the point of starvation. In a .little room unrelieved by even a picture, I lay for months, but the doctor in his compassion paid for the loan of books from the Mechanics Institute. I began to read books of travel and obtained quite an education in foreign lore. The doctors expressed the opinion that I should never recover in the penned-up cottage in Old Road and I was taken to a cottage opposite to the Horse and Jockey, a spot which was then considered, and indeed was a' country' place. But the journey in a coach shook me so severely that I determined not to return in it. A happy notion came to me. I knew that the Heath family had a .Sedan chair in which a consumptive son used to be carried to Arpley Meadows for fresh air at the times of the incoming tide. The loan of this was solicited and I made the journey back in this old-fashioned and stately con- veyance. But, alas, the old chair had not, been dusted for years, and I arrrived in town dignified but nearly .as • black as a chimney .sWeep.

My unfortunate loss conferred indirectly another honour. - It had given me the distinction of being the first Warringtonian to inhale chloroform; now I gained the glory of being the last native to ride in a Sedan chair.

Standing one day in Bridge-street to shelter from a shower I was called across the street by Mr. Edward Twiss who,inspired by a kind interest, offered to pay for two quarters' schooling for me at Brewtnall's school in Newton-street. I gladly accepted, and though a young man of 21 or 22 was put in the children's classes and had to work my way up. This I quickly did and was soon at the top. My half year tuition being ended I applied to the Guardians (Mr. Beamont being chairman) for a grant of 4d. per week for school money. This was given, and assisted by a gift of books from Mr. William Alcard and Mr. Edmund Robinson I got back to my studies. After nine months I began work at Blundell's coal yard at a wage of 4s. per week, which was gradually raised to 7s. 6d. I contrived to live upon is. per week, and kept it up for weeks in suc- cession. Though walking four miles a day on crutches to my Work, I felt no inconveni- ences from my spare diet. Certainly there was no suffering from indigestion! A coal strike having almost destroyed the business I sought work -elsewhere and took a situa- tion in Widnes at a remuneration of 2s. per week and food.

I was approaching my thirtieth year when I drifted into the buying and selling of fents. With a capital of 30s. I opened business in the Warrington market. It was sorely against the dignity of a de Clare (for I claim to be descended from Gilbert de Clare a cousin of William the Conquerer) to set up in the rag market,but I faced the situation as bravely as possible and after 12 years spent thus was able to retire. My wants have been few and my hobbies numerous and I have been able to live in comfort. Like Joseph Bretherton I could say "my riches consist not in the abundance of my possessions, but in the fewness of my wants,"

The editor asks for recollections of my early days. My readers may be surprised to hear that I remember the first Warrington election in 1832. I was then a child 21 years old and my mother carried me to witness the nomination. The hustings were fixed in the triangular space at the junction of Butter- market-street and Dial-street and I remem- ber the cries of " Hornby for ever." I well remember the building of Warrington Bridge though I cannot recall its predecessor. The circumstance is fixed on my mind by the fact that when the temporary bridge was being demolished I fell into a pool left by the excavations and was in danger of drowning.

My father was a Chartist and in my early days I used to attend the meetings held in a room in Union-street. Here I heard Fergus O'Connor, Ernest Jones and many others of the fiery orators of the Charter. I well remember Captain Leigh who has been mentioned in recent issues of your paper 1 recollect • seeing him travel round the town in his coffin (a boat-like thing on wheels) and John Stirrup of Mersey-street sitting therein and playing a fiddle as they went. Captain Leigh wore a pig-tail and knee breeches and tradition had it that he had chosen a certain river bay called Knotts hole as his watery grave. I enjoyed the fliendship of the late Lord Winmarlegh who often sought me out in the market. We engaged in happy and animated conversations, for I was then a zealous Radical, and I well remember ou r discussion of the Reform Bill when it was before the country, and our frequent debates upon matters of home and foreign politics. I remember Peter Phillips well and his wife Hannah and brother Joshua.l was on the best terms also with Philip Carpenter mentioned in your last issue. They might well call him" Water Rat "for he had a great love of bathing. He went out with his friends to the canals to swim and gave them degrees according to merit. I was one of his happy followers and attained the distinction of an M.A. degree. I was a member too of his total abstinence society, and remember that the rules were so rigid that when I had my operation and was or.dered some wine I had to have my name taken temporarily riff the toll. But no doubt this will be quite as much of my story as you will care for in one instalment.

OUR MISCELLANY.

We are glad to present this week the Life Story of Mr. Joseph Clare. Though the stories are a mere outline ancl only represent a fraction of the real life we think they will b 3 found to have - permanent value and interest. We ought perhalis to add that Mr. Clare has read widely and been a; collector of books. A few years ago 'he presented a valuable collection to the Warrington Library. Mr. Clare until recently was a disciple of Isaac Walton and has doubtless spent his happiest hours in the congenial company of fellow fishermen on the banks of the pools and streams of Cheshire.

** * *

The memory of the suffering entailed in the operation he underwent sixty years ago remains with him, and he has arranged to make a gift of .£2OO to the Nursing Institution of Warrington in the hope that the agonies of future sufferers may be alleviated thereby. A similar gift goes to St. Mark's Hospital, London, in which he had an operation in 1882. r

We have been reading with some interest Mr. Rockefeller's autobiography published under the title " Random reminiscences of men and events." America has sent us some curious pieces of literature, and this is one of them. It is delightful to see the multi- millionaire posing for posterity, and assuring us of his hearty dislike of speculation and money getting. He tells that he knows us of nothing more despicable than money-getting for money's sake, and tlisapproves of specula- tion on the high ground that the only thing which is of lasting benefit to a man is what he does for himself, sentiments which we pre- sume Mr. Rockefeller proclaims to the Sunday school of which he is the distinguished head.

** * *

Much more truly Rockefeller-an is a story which his proper business pride betrays him into telling. He had arranged to place twelve ships upon the great inland lakes. The order was a large one in those days, and there was a fear of having to pay very high prices. This is how the astute financier dealt with the matter : Mr. Mather, his agent, was entrusted with the negotiations, and took every care that the number of ships he wished to construct remained a secret. He sent his plans and specifications—each a duplicate of the others—to the ship building firms, and asked each to tender for one or two ships. All naturally supposed that at most only two ships were to be built, and each was eager to get the work, or at least one of the two vessels.

** * *

On the day before the contracts were to be let, all the bidders. were taken one by one into Mr. Mather's private office for special conference covering all the details prepara- tory for the final bid. At the appointed hour the bids were in. Peep was the interest on the part of the gentlemen as to who would be the lucky one to draw the prize. Mr. Mather's manner had convinced each that somehow he himself must be the favoured bidder, yet when he came to meet his com- petitors in the hotel lobby the beams of satisfaction which lit up their faces compelled many anxieties.

** * *

At last the deciding hour came, and at about the same moment each representative received a little note from Mr. Mather con- veying to him the tidings that to him had been awarded a contract sufficient to supply lils works to the utmost capacity. They all rushed with a dommon impulse to the hotel lobby where they had been accustomed to meet, each bent on displaying his note and commiserating his unsuccessful rivals, only to discover that each had a contract for all be could do and that each had been actually bidding against nobody but himself.

** * *

[Harry Gastonard tells Paddy O'Connell of his visit to Madame Lead She opened her eyes in iTild alarm at this. 1 had seen her angry before, hilt never as she was angry now. "Fear!" she cried, raising herself upon a. frail arm, and making no attempt to modu- late the shrill echo of her alarm. "Of what, then, am I afraid, Monsieur Gastonard?" "You are afraid of discovery, Madame d'Aleneon."

A washerwoman 'complain- ing at the tub, a virago at the doors of a tavern had not been a spectacle less repul- sive.

I kept my temper; the stake Wa6, beyond all belief, momentous to me. 'lVere never was, Paddy, such a wild arrow shot in all this world before, and never will be again, I do believe. Nothing but a. dogged faith in my own convictions could have bent such a bow. This woman had sent for me: her manner sufficiently declared her embar- rassment. Unless she had something to offer me, my visit must end in her discomfiture. Lea d'Alencon is not the woman to bring such an affront upon herself. This I per- ceived, and in my mad desire for the truth oould have knelt at her very feet, and im- plored her to aid me. She knew—the key was locked in the safe of her intrigues. My God! "What a torture to say as much, and to realise my own impotence! Well, the shot was fired, and the target touched. She had lhitened to me with her t?es wide open, and her mouth pursed up, as though anger were held at bay a little while by reflection. When she spoke, her voice hail lost its shrill timbre of protest, and all its pleasing qualities been regained. _

"We are-all foolish sometimes, -Mon3icor Gastonard. • I was fooli6h when I counted you among the number of my friends. Let us not speak of it. Very well ; I do know something, and you shall know it--the dead Count was your wife's lover; that is what I know, Monsieur Gastonard."

"It ia a lie," said I. And I leitned back in my chair, and watched her critically. "So poor a lie that 6o clever a. woman a 6 Madame Lea should not have told it."

She turned her eyes sway from me, and continued her infamous story, unabashed and unashamed.

• "It is a lie," I repeated—but her words held me to my chair as though an unknown hand caught me by the throat. "Why do you tell me so foolish a. lie, Madame Lea?" She rose and came across to me. The spell of her mendacity was broken.

"I tell it because I loved you," she said. "Yes, ves, yes, it is the 'truth, and no lie. I loved you, and you left me for .this .crea- tare, this canaille, this girl of the. fetes and the circus. I turned upon my heel, and left her. She had not moved from the place when I passed out. I could see that a word would have un- sealed her lips and cast her, a mendicant for pity, at my feet. CHAPTER XXXI'.

It was after midnight when I went out to meet a chill night, with a drizzle of hos- tile rain which 'drove the people from the boulevards and sent the loafers to the baser cafes. For my part, although there were cabs at the chiors of the Ritz, I determined to walk to my hotel. So many strange thoUghts came to me, so many hopes, so many fears have been my portion, that I have learned to dread the constraint of rooms and turn to the liberty of streets and the darkness. Here, under God's sky, be there a heaven of stars cr a veil of cloud, I may still believe that my little wife is look- ing upward, that hr eyes are cleaving tnc nignt as mine, and that the same prayer' which I breathe is also upon her lips. Ah, Paddy, will it ever be that I shall wake again "to find her pillowed head npoi my arm, to know tha I have won her loVc and will keep it to the end? If Paris would but answer me that—the mocking crowds, the darkened canopy of night, the unknown voiees which torment me! Shall to-morrow be as yesterday and all the morrows after Oh, God forbid ! --I cannot lose her; I not cease to hope that even as she came t me in ,this city of my youth, so shall Paris surrender her now in the hour of my . need.— Your friend,

HARRY G ASTCNARD.

I had closed tlits letter. but must open it again. Jules Farman brings me a strange piece of news. It may mean much or little. He followed me, it seems, to the Ritz last night believing that I also had been followed now for some days in Paris.. He had waited some twenty minutes in the Place Vendome, when a man passed him whom lie recogn'sed. It was our .old friend the famous ruffian Jean- le-Mont, from the old Café of the Assassins. The man lingered a little while outside the Ritz, and then went on toward the Boule- vards.

Now, what -does this mean —what does your wisdom make of it? Jules Farman will say nothing. He ho s been very silent these laA few days. Is it pos&ble that our first ideas are to be justified? I begin to believe so, even if the light 1)e (Inn and the path uncer- tain. Tell me what you think and do not fail to write to me. I am very IGnely, Paddy. There is not a man in all the world. so wist- ful of sympathey as your friend Harry to- . .

Simple Injurics with _Serious Results

NEWTON COLLIER KILLED.

A "lusher-on " named J. Brady, who resided in •Scot-lane, Newton, was at work at the Meadow Pit, last Friday, when he was accidentally jammed between two tubs, and received injuries which resulted in his death.

PROTECTION AND EMPLOYMENT.

LABOUR'S HANDICAP IN AMERICA.

[By MR. HI

A very appropriate sub-heading to the above may well be " A Tory red herring." The Tory party seem to hold a monopoly .of all the good things of this world. If they only get hold of the good ,things that are supposed to be in store in the world to cOlllO how blessed it must be to be a member of that happy grasping family. I cannot just say which place this party holds in most esteem —Heaven or the House of Lords, but it holds a tight grasp on the latter, and as for the former the injunction that Dr. Clore wished to place upon ('mien Hensley Henson's actions would imply that the Tory party, which is the Church party, will, at least, make,a strong claim to all the front seats, and never for a moment doubting the claim will be honoured. We have the word of Holy Writ for it that " the earth is the Lord's and the fulness thereof." and we have the Tories' word for the correct interpre- tatiou of this passage, as we have for that other passage : "The poor ye have with ye always." At the present time of writing there seem to be two qpwstions before the British public and both of .them have in the years that are past, been written about and spoken about times without number ; and one, though dead, will not stay beneath the sod, and the other, will, like the poor be always with us. I_, refer to Protection and war. Protection is a clean cut Ivord, oven if it carries no clear meaning with it, an -I so its expounders have found when attempting to lick it into shape so as to makt it acceptable as a slogan. One is reminded here of the anecdote Mr. Will Crooks delighted the House With recently. He pictured a worker and a voter meeting, the *orker laying to his friend, " Come in here and have a drink, we want eight Dreadnoughts, and we won't wait— give him a drink landlord,—they won't cost you any- thing, as a tax on foreign goods will provide all the money—another drink for my friend, -please—and then .by taxing the foreigner we shall keep all his goods out and there will be work for every one !" Then, after a pause, Mr. Crooks gravely remarked to the speaker " Why, Sir, you must ,get the man. half drunk, and keeii him so or he won't understand the argument." I have just at hand, a. book written in America, and by an American. It gives the history of the Chartist movement, and compels the reader's interest to read on through the Repeal of the Corn Laws movement. It title is, "How. England averted a revolution of force." I could quote from this book; but \dna is the use. Do men get 60 used to party . that they cannot break away? Can they so ignore the lessons that history gives them that they become blind to the fact that they are following the false trail of a red herring and are gaping with open .mouths at the tricks of a political juggler? This may be so. What were Mr. Chamberlain's own words? They arc definite. They are, as he has told us, a system of "scientific taxation" to be substituted for "profitlets tamtien." lhey include, to use his own words, "a small protection against foreign manufactured goods." 'They are embodied in a tariff, which would levy two shillings a quarter on foreign corn, five per cent. cn foreign meat and dairy produce, and "ten per cent. on the average all manufactured goods." They treat India as a- hireign ccuntry not belonging to the Empire. "I put that (India) aside," said Mr. Chamberlain," and I consider only our relations to our own kinsfolk, to that whits British population that constitutes the majority in the great belf-governing Colonies of _

That was Tariff Reform. What is it now?. Mr. Balfour, and the unknown quantity have asked this question a many times during the past few months. The quest;mi became so serious indeed, that unless the answer ezime,. and quick, his Majesty's Opposi- tion would become an unntanatrheable mob. It came; and instead of being represented by the word Dread- everything it was the boastful Dreadnought. Let us throw the experiences of the past aside. Don't for one moment believe the open letter written by the German Michel to • joint. Bull and printed in the Contemporary Review. 1 nuty, in this letter and one to follow, say some unpalatable things, neither acceptable to the Editor nor yet to the reader. All the same they are true. England, and let us say Great Britain, has ceased to be the workshop of the world. She first sold the fruits of her mechanical genius to the United States, awl now she. sends her most skilful mechanics, fifty more of whom have just arrived. Their spokesman says - -" We are all good mechanics and as 'a rule should get along best in our country, but there is no work for us there, and we have come to America to become citizens." Another item. The Lorain Steel Company of America has, in competition with British and Oersttan rail makers, carried off the Glasgow municipal street car rail contract. Four- ye:irs ago a British- Standards Committee with Sir B. Baker at its head recommended rail makers in Great Britain to confine themselves to the rolling of certain sections, and consumers gave that (recision their cordial support. As soon .as the findings of the Committee were issued the American makers at once laid down the required rolling plant, while British makers have continued 1, roll their own sections ; hence the Americans are now enabled to obtain every order of any magnitude which .comes in the market. The head of the Glasgow municipal car service says : There is now no proS- peel, of a single ton of rails being given to British makers unless they at once lay down the necessary plant ; Americans must continue to secure all 'the, orders." This is dumping if you like. 1 ask where does the chameleon-hued Tariff Reform come in ? .

Let 1110 point out another feature in this question, one that may have curious results. It is most certainly America that is invading England, and she will succeed in that object ; that , object being her destiny. You have now the American Department Store in London. That means you have American inetluxls, and they may make you get a move on. You have at least two Americans who have become masters of two packs of foxhounds. You have several born Americans who are quietly nursing Conservative con- stituencies, and I cannot at the moment say how many American rich women you have who are politicians, and have homes on both sides the Atlantic. I venture' to say that before another five years have passed over England vihl for ever have buried the Taff Reform question. lam not a Free Trader in the sense the Liberal party uses the phrase, but I dearly love my native land and all the time I am trying to stifle this feeling of love, of patriotism, of nationality I also know that the best way to achieve my object is the only way, law and order.

Now allow me to give a few samples of Protection A traveller crossed the channel, and said :---

No sooner had we crossed the Channel than we discovered how much they lose who have not. our English privilege of Free Trade. Boys on the railway platform were asking 10d. for a cup of tea for which in England we would pay 3d., and think • that a good price. I took up a newspaper rag at a bookstall—not half as large as" The Daily News " —and offered a half-penny for it, bat found the ' price to be three-halfpence. With lower wages, longer hours, and dearer commodities, how can the otcctive counlries excite British envy or tempt the Englishman to follow their example? Your humble servant crossed the ocean and in the United States has had some very unpleasant experi- ences. To-day the average number of beggars coming to my .door is about three, and I can tell you they are not bums," they are fairly well-dressed. Yesterday an old lady stopped me on the street and ..atiked for a nickel. I guessed her at •70. Two weeks 'ago I Met an old shop mate, one with whom 'lliad worked at least a dozen years. We had a talk. He had done no work for over twelve months. While we were talking another 'man came up. He had done no work during the past six months. . These two men, and I may add anotherean older man than myself whom I visited a week ago, are all honest, sober and as hard workers as any employers need wish for. Each of these men, and we could have found hundreds more, had just one reason to give, and one I know to be true, they were of the wrong nationality and the wrong religion. New corners of the right nationality and the right religion got the jobs as they turned up. I have worked with thousands of men who for over twenty years were never at liberty to make a friendly appointment during week evenings, nor did we ever count on having a Sunday to our- selves. The results of our toil are now crossing and do cross the ocean every summer to be spent in ".doiug" Europe ! Hurrah for Protection and liberty. Pro- tection as it works in the United States is one of tlmi. most unjust, greed-breeding and unrighteous institutions the mind of man ever worked out ! The following I take from to-day's paper. It is a short scene as enacted in the American House of Lords, the Senate.

SEASIDE HOTEL FIRE. - Ladies Injured Jumping from Windows. MR. AND MRS. ARMITAGE'S PROVIDENTIAL ESCAPE.

There was great excitement in the little Cornish town of Mullion on Sunday, when the Polurrian Hotel was destroyed by tire, several persons being injured. The outbieak occurred early in the morning, ann. within half an hour the building was. completelY destroyed, nothing but the walls remaining. The fir° originated in the servants' quarters, and it was one ef the maids who gave the alarm. Her cries ef " Fire ! " roused the whole hotel, and inunediatelY the building 'became the scene of the wildest excite- ment. Visitors in their night attire, and hardly more than half awake, rushed from their bedrooms into the corridor on learning that the hotel was on lire. Some made their escape at once, while others rushed bark into their rooms to save, if possible, some of their effects. Some of the visitors, Ulf crazy with taro!, without waiting to see if they • were cut off front llie stairs by the flames, jumped fram the second and third floor windows tothe ground. Fortunately no one Wa'' fatally injured, but a Mrs. Hohne, in jumping fromt a balcony, severely injured her spine, whilst Miss. Harrison, another visitor, had both legs broken, sud Miss Carey Tonkin and Miss Jane Gilbert, two of Ow staff, were badly burned. Miss Tonkin, althoo gli almost. sidrocated, managed to reach the terrace, and there fell exhausted. She was discovered mid rescued

from her perilous position by Mr. A. Gilbert, a visitor. Doctors were wired for at the Lizard, Ilelston, and Redruth to attend the injured, who iwere removed IP neighbouring bungalows, while the visitors who were unhurt were taken to a neighbouring hotel.

Plight of a pretty little victim of St Vitu3' Dance.

Every trace of the disorder dispelled

by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills

P.C. John Preston, stationed at Stockton Heath, sa;d that he received information that a disturbance among the Irishmen had broken out, at the Ring o' Bells at Daresbury. Witness proceeded with P.C.'s Woolley and Catterall to Booth's Farm, Preston o'th' Hill, and there arrested prisoner and six other men, the latter having been dealt with on another chargo. The prisoners ere arreste3 in their shanty, the door having to be burst open. The stick was found hidden in the shanty chimney. Witness conveyed the prisoners to Stockton Heath police station, and on the following morning Mrs. Phillips accompanied witness there and identified prisoner from among fifteen men. Witness cautioned him and charged him with assaulting Mr. Phillips, to which he made no reply.—Prisoner now said, "I have nothing to say.l was the worse for drink." —Prisoner was committed to Chester Sessions.

Benjamin Fox, Ludlow Cottage, Hadley, was sum- moned with travelling from Healey to Dunham Massey. on the 29th April without a ticket, also with travelling the rt turn journey without a ticket, with intent to avoid payment. Mr. Heddleston, of Euston, prosecuted, and Mr. Farrah, of Manchester, defended.—Mr. Huddleaton stated that the defendant had been in the habit of travelling between Healey and Dunham Massey, and the station master at Heatley had become suspicious, and on the 29th of April he sent a porter with too same train as the defendant travelled by to are if he left the train at Dunham Massey which the defendant did not do. The same porter also met a return train at Dunham Massey but the defendant did not join it there. On defendant's return to Heatley the station master questioned defendant as to where he joined the train, and he replied "at Dunham Massey." After being further questioned he admitted having joined the train at Broadheath, and offered to pay the excess fare.— William Bradburn, a porter at Broadheath, spoke to travelling with the defendant, who did not leave the train at Dunham Massey on the morning, and did not join it at night —Walter Timperley,station master, who described the affair as one of the biggest frauds that ever came under his notice, said that on defendant arriving at Heatley, he asked him where he left and joined the train. Defendant at once said "Dunham Massey." Witnass thereupon replied " I have proofs that you did not do that," and defendant finally admitted that he had left and joined the train at Broad- heath. Defendant wanted witness to accept the excess fare, but witness said he would have to report him. To this, defendant said "I would rather pay £lO than have you report the matter, and let my employers get to know." Witness said he would have to report the mattar to head quarters. Defendant also admitted committing the same fraud on the 25th, 26th and 27th of the same month. Richard Fandbach, another porter, corroborated the conversation between defendant and the last witness.— Defendant in his evidence said that on the thy in question he was asked at Heatley Station by a railway official to travel with him, and so he went on to Broad- heath, Heabtolutely dented the conversation with the stationmaster,and in the beat of the monn nt defendant thought that he was going to be prosecutei for travel- ling in a second class carriage with the railway official. A third class return half from Dunham Massey to Heatley was then produced, on examination Mr. Timperley said that the ticket bad been purchased after defendant was caught. Mr. Timperley wanted to know if defendant had tbe ticket when he was caught, why did he not turn it up instead of pleading for inercy.—Defendant said he had the ticket but was a bit —stupid and would not turn it up.—Mr. Huddlestcn asked for the ci.se to be remanded in order to trace the ticket but Mr. Farrah thought it was not necessary. A letter was read from defendant to the headquarters at Warrington ple :ding that no proiecution would follow.—The 13,•nch adjourned and on their return the Chairman said they were bound to iegister a conviction and the defendant would be fined 10s. and costs in eleh case.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

DEATH OF MR. JOHN HIIDDLESTON. The death took place last Friday of Mr. John Huddleston, of Earle-street, Earlestown. Ihe deceased, nho was 42 years of age, had been a member of the Earlestown Viaduct Prize Band for ove.t 20 years, He was also well known as a comedian, and his services were always in great demaod for help in the cause of charity. '1 ho deceased leaves a wife and two childred. He was a brother to Huddleston, the Lancashire county cricketer.

EARLESTOWN BOY DROWNED.

A sad drovrning case occurred at Earlestown on Monday night. Fred Percival, eight years of age, Eon of Thomas Percival, of London Row, Vitriol-rquare, Earlestown, employed as a charge hand at tie Sankey Sugar Works, w4s gathering flowers in the neighbour- hood of the Sankey Canal. After being brought home by his mother, the boy took the first opportunity of returning to the canal, and it is supposed that ho fell down the bank into the water and was drowned. After grappling for an hour the body was recovered. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held the inquest at the Earlestown Town Hall on Thursday morning. The evidence was to the effr,ct that in company wit h another little boy named Dickson deceased went out on Monday night intending to gather flowers in Burton- wood. Twenty minutes later Dickson returned home carrying Percival's wet cap, and informed his mother that Fred" was in the canal.—lnformation was giver, to the police, and grappling operations immediately commenced, with the result that the body was eventu- ally recovered near the Sankey swing bridge, life being extinct.--A verdict of "Accidental desth" was returned the jury expressing their sympathy with the unfortunate boy's parents.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WARRINGTON HAWKER s TROUBLE.—Arthur Lea, Old-gardens, Winwick-road, Warrington, was summoned at the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday for peddling without a certificate. Detective Davies said the prisoner had been coming to the town with a rag and bone cart, assisting a female hawker. He had warned him, some limo ago, that be required a certificate.— Defendant, who stated that he. only received is. 3d. and Is: 6d. per day from the woman for his service i,was fined 2s. 6d. and advised to take out a certificate at once.

RUNCORN'S MAY QUEEN.

On Saturday afternoon Ruttcorn celebrated the crown- ing of the May Queen. The Queen was Miss Ethel May Duckett, daughter of Councillor Duckett, wno played the part with grace and dignity. The procession was witnessed by thousands of spectators„ the town being thronged by visitors from the surrounding district. There were many tableaux and characters from history and nursery rhymes. Afterwards there were dances an other performances on the football ground. The Queen was crowned by Miss Kathleen Carter, daughter of Mr. H. R. Carter, treasurer to the District Council, in the presence of about 5,C00 people. The proceeds are devoted to the Cottage Hospital and other charitits. ,

FUNERAL OF' A WIDNES MAGISTRATE.

The interment took place on Saturday afternoon, at Farnworth Parish Churchyard, of the late Mr.Benjamiu .Brown, aged seventy-four, a well-known leader in Non- conformist circles,and a director of the Widnes Foundry Company. Only recently be went to reside in Garston. The funeral service was held in Farnworth Wesleyan Chapel, and was conducted by the Rev. T. Galland Hartley, chairman of th 3 Liverpoor district, in the presence of a large congregation. The Rev. J. E. Dixon, of Garston,gave an impressive address. Amongst those at the waveside were the niyor, aldermm, and councillors, and representatives of the public, religious and business life of Widnes. The wreaths included one from the workmen at the Widms Foundry.

WIDNES LIBERALS' ACTIVITY.

The first of a series of outdoor Liberal meetings in the Widnes Parliamentary Division was held on Trusday forenoon outside the Wireworks at Prescot. Mr. Max Muspratt presided, and Mr. Fred Horne, late Parliamentary candidate for the Ludlow Division of Shropshire, was the chief rpeaker. The meeting was heNI on the ground opposite the works, and was attended by about 500 workpeople, who listened attentively to speeches on the Bu.lget and the old age pension scheme. A supplementary meeting was also held in the evening at Crontcn, which was also attended by a gcod number of villagers. Mr. Glover took the chair, and the meet- ing was addressed by Mr. Fred Home and Mr. Callie, secretary of the Financial Reform Association.

COLLIERY FATALITY INQUEST.

Mr. Brighouse held an. inquiry on Saturday. at Rain- ford, near St. Helens, relative to the dtath of Henry Mather, aged eighteen, a &Wier at the Rainford Colliery, and living at Millbrook, Eccleston. Mather had only been at work in the mine for the past fortnight. On Friday morning last a man named Thompson and Mather were following a journey of tubs when the latter started to run to get past the tubs. Thompson shouted to him not to pass, but he took no notice, and whilst attempting to get past the tubs at the narrow place he was jammed and killed instantly. A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned, and the jury added that in their opinion Thompson was not a fit and proper person to instruct Mather.

VITRIOL OUTRAGE AT ST. HELENS.

At St. Helens, on Sunday night, William Helsby, 15, South John-street, Parr, was terribly burned and had one of his eyes destroyed by a quantity of vitriol thrown in his face. The cruel act is supposed to have been committed by flelsby'ii stepson, a young man vamed Joseph Westwell, who recently came out of prison after serving a senterme of four moths for stealing his step- father's watch and chain. About sewn o'clock, Westwell went to Helsbys' home and told his mother he was going to the back kitchen .to wash his hands. A few minutes afterwards he returned to the living room with a tumbler in his hand, ant threw the contents into the faco of his stepfather, who was seated at the table having his tea. The stepfather at once showed symptoms of extreme pain, and it was evident that the liquid thrown at him was vitriol or some other acid. Assistance was leadered, and in the meantime Wotwell left the house and Helsby was at once hurried off to Dr. O'Keefe's surgery, where it was found that the right eye bad been destroyed. The whole of his face and neck was shockingly burned, and his clothing was partly destroyed. Dr. O'Keefe treated the injuries and des- ratched the suffering man to the Liverpool Eye Infirmary. In the meantime, the police were informed and a vigorous search was instituted for the arrest of Hel:by's assailant.

ACCUSED BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES. Westwell was arrested in the neighbourhood of Prescot during the night. He was brought before the St. Felons magistrates on Monday. The Chief Constable said this was a very bad case, and he had to ask the Bench to grant a remand. Westwell was charged with throwing vitriol into tie face of his stepfather and absolutely destroying the sight of one eye, and in all probability the sight or the other. When prisoner saw the detective come in he said, " Hello, is that you, Anders? I haye been expect- ing you." Andtrs told him he was to Le arrested, and cautioned him,but be said, "Is it for vitro] throwing?" Later he &sled if he had blinded Helsby' and when he was told that one eye was blinded he said, " I'll blind the -in the other whTn I come out. I suppose I shall have to go to the assrAes." When be was charged at the St. Helens Police Office he made no reply.

Anders gave formal evidence, and said tivitlielsby's clothing was all burnt. Defendant All I have got to say is that the vitriol was not kin ghiss, and I didn't ask for a wash. The Clerk told him to reserve wlut he had to say, :nd a week's remand wls granted.

PERFECTION TRADEMARK CASE.

Case for Messrs. Lever Brothers.

r. Justice Swinfen Eady, in the Chancery Division on Moneav, continued the hearing of arnotion on behalf of Joseph *Crosfleld, Ltd., Warrington, asking the Court, notwithstanding the opposition of Mes-rs. Lever Brothers, Ltd., Port Sunlight, to direct the Registrar of Trademarks to proceed with the registration of the applicants' trademark "Perfection" as applied to household soaps. Mr. Astbury, in opening the case on behalf of nes3rs. Lever Bros. Ltd., said that Mr. Lever, in opposition the registrAion of the word "Perfection." was not anxious or desirous in any way to derive benefit from theareputstion of Crosfield's Ltd. Mr. Lever held a strong view that it. was undesirable, in the Soap trade or' any other trace, that adjectival or cornmendntory terms should be registered under the Act of 1905. In his opinion, the registration of such words would lead to confusion. If the word "Perfection" were registered every little grocer alto used the word "Perfection" in an adjectival sense would have the onus cast upon him of showing whether what he. had done was not calcu- lated to prejudice Messrs. Crosfield's, instead of the onus, as at present, being cast upon Messrs. Crosfield's. Evidence would be called to show (list whrn ctiston ers asked for Crosfield's "Perfection" they looked for the picture of a pyramid on the packets, tad not merely for the word " Perfection." _ _ _ .

- Evidence WPS called in support of Lever Bros.' Limited, opposition to registration. Further evidence was given on Tttesd ty. Mr. W. H. Lever, M.P., gusetiosed as to an inter- view at Liverpool between himself, the two Messrs. Crosfield and Mr. Timmis, denied th:lt he used any threats to Messrs. Crosfield. The object of the meet- ing was to discuss matters in connection with the soap trade at Bristol. He had no recollection of having at that meeting contrasted commendatory and adjective! words with other word.:. The weakness of such words was that there was no trademark. He had never threatened to use or imitate the words or" get-up "of Messrs. Crosfield's goods. He did not wish, however, wit'i regard to theluture, to have his action crippled. Iu er,ss-examination, Mr. Lever said honest trading was the keynote of his business. He had always zealously protected his shareholder%from the invasions of other ncople. Somebody attempted to register the word " Sundew," and Messrs. Crosfield supported his company's oppesiticn to the registration. The hearing was adjouaned. JUDGMENT RESERVED.

The hearing of the action was concluded on 'Thurs- day after hearing counsel's arguments. Mr. Justice Eady reserved judgment.

SOCIE ry.

The Council has now arranged the list of classes for this year's show which is to be held on July 13th, 14tb, and 15th. Competition in the district classes in the shire horse section has been'thrown open to any exhibit belonging to a resident within 25 miles from the show yard, and several classes have' been add€ d in which competition is comcnied to bona-fide tenant farmers residing in the hundred of WirrAl. The rrizes for weight carrying bunters have been increased, further two classes are being provided for four-in-hand coaching teams, viz., "park "team and " road " tam. As in previous years there ale a number of classes for saddle and harness horses in addition to these for hackneys to be sham in baud. The leaping competi- tions are handsomely provided for and in order that the prizes, of Ahich there are four for each day, may be the better distributed, the first prize winner on the first driy.cannot again compete, neither can .the first prize winner On the second day. An extensive classifi- cation has been provided for shorthorn Cattle including two classes in which the competition is confined to tenant farmers in the hundred of Wirral. Some slight alteration has been made in the classes for dairy cattle, while classes are being again included for Jersey and Kerry cattle, and it is quite possible tbe prize list may yet see clasies added for these useful milkers, six., "Dexter Kerries." In the sheep section Shropshires and long% °oiled varieties are again being handsomely catered for as are a'so the various breeds of pigs. A number of the Society's gold, silver, and bronze medals are offered for machinery and implement", &c. The same classification as last year is provided for cheese, batter and eggs. A representative classification for dogs is again provided and some thirty challenge pr:zes being offcred by the Kennel Club, this being a championship show, but the dogs will only be on exhibition during tbe second and ill:1.d days of the show. An extensive classification for poultry pigeons, rabbits, and envies has been. arranged and stu,tild attract largo entries. The Society's handsome challenge cups and. trophies will be again offered for competition in the various sections, in &Withal to several valuable cups to be won outright. The prize schedules will be issued in a few weeks and till be sent to all who have exhibited at this Society's show during the,Past three years, other intending exhibitors should apply for a copy to the Secretary at 6,Hamiltoll Square.

The King's Visit.

Sir,—lt has occurred to me that it would be a beautiful sight to see every house decorated with window boxes of flowers along the route of the Royal procession on the occasion of the visit of their Majesties the King and Queen to our good old town. I understand their Majesties will enter the town from the Manchester-road and will proceed along Manchester-road, Church-street, Buttermarket-street, Sankey-street, to the Town Hall, and after the address of welcome and loyalty has been presented to them by the Mayor the Royal procession will proceed along Sankey-street and Liverpool-road to Knowsley. The distance from one end of the town to the other is about 21 miles. I hope my suggestion will meet with the Approval of the residents and shopkeepers along the route and trust that the Beautiful Warrington Society will take the matter up.—Yours faithfully,

LOYALIST

' A second letter which we have received from the Secretary of the Beautiful Wm rington Society makes a similar suggestion to the townspeople. He offers to give inquirers any information as to the cost of providing and stocking the window- boxes if they will communicate with him at the Old Academy.
