===== 0002601_19090522_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr Howes and .t 5.

Sir,—l have taken an interest in, the letters that lave appeared in your columns from time to time under the above head. As one who was present lam amazed at the attitude of Mr. Howes. In your last issue he talks about a bet. As a matter of fact there never was any such thing as a bet. Mr. J. Royle's version is absolutely correct and Mr. J. Howes is entirely, wrong. Of course this may be accounted for by the terrible condition of agitation he was in at the time. Mr. Royle first offered to give £1 to four poor families, if a statement he had read from a document -was not true. Mr. Howes immediately said he would give £5 if Mr. Royle would prove his statement. Mr. Royle at once accepted the challenge. This, Sir, is exactly what happened and I fail to see what the Tariff Reform League of either Warrington or any- -where else can have to do with a matter of this kind. Is it not rather a subterfuge on the part of Mr. Howes to get out of an awkward position ?—I am, &c., J. BRATHERTON.

47, Thurlow St., Salford May 18th, 1909.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Lymm Tradesmen's Association and Citizens' Sunday.

Sir,--In reading your report of above, two questions must present themselves to the minds of many; viz. : Is there not an Urban District Council in Lymm, and is there not a Nonconformist place of -worship in Lymm? Why should an unrepresentative body like the Tradesmen's Association take upon itself to arrange a public celebration of this kind. It seems to me that this association does not realize its proper functions. As a Trade Protection Society it can do and has done, ,good work, but why it should think itself qualified to act as a representative body of the citizens,- I cannot understamd'. Surely the proper course for such an association to take would be to requisition the District Council to inaugurate a Citizen Sunday. Furthermore why should it be taken for granted that such a service should be held in an Anglican Church. I submit, that it ought to be herd alternately in a Nonconformist and an Anglican Church. I trust the District Council, even now, will take the matter up and if we are to have a Citizen Bunday let it be one in fact, as well as in name, aud not merely a tradesmen's Sunday parade.—l am, yours, &c., NONCONFORMIST CITIZEN. Lymm. May 19th, 1909.

===== 0002601_19090522_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Lady Greenall Versus Mrs. Asquith

Sir,—lt is very amusing to notice the eagerness of Lady Greenall to make political capital out of a private function," Afternoon tea to lady friends and a display of French dresses." It can hardly be expected that everyone will approve Mrs. Asquith's action which, I venture to suggest, was quite innocently carried out and I am quite sure no one will regret its effect more than the distinguished lady herself. Lady Greenall may have rendered her party some little service by drawing local attention to this unfortunate incident and I venture very respectfully to suggegt to her Ladyship that she can render our Warrington tradesmen valuable service by forming a local league, the object of which should be that its members patronise our local tradespeople instead of the huge London and provincial Stores. Lady Greenall is doubtless aware that many of our leading families who make their money in and around Warrington purchase most of their requirements out of town. This is infinitely worse for the town in -which Lady Greenall professes to take so deep an interest. I appeal to Lady Greenall to take the lead in this matter and thus set an example which will earn for her the gratitude of many a struggling trades- man in the good old town of Warrington, to which Lady Greenall and her family owe so much.— Yours, etc„ A WARRINGTON TRADESMAN. Warrington. May 19th, 1909.

MRS. ASQUITH'S EXPLANATION.

Mrs. Asquith has written the following letter to Mr. Richardson, the member for South Nottingham "10. Downing-street, Whitehall, S.W., May 20, 190 g. "Dear Mr. Richardson,—l must thank you for your very kind letter. You can assure your friends that there is no truth in the statement that there has been an exhibition or sale of foreign stuffs or clothes at 10, Downing-street. On the 6th of this month, in the course of the afternoon, I received in any private rooms at tea from twenty to twenty- five of my personal friends, and a well-known French costumier, whose models can be bought in any London shop, brought some specimens for the inspection of myself and my guests. It was a purely private occasion. Yours sincerely, MARGARET ASQUITH."

===== 0002601_19090522_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Open-air Politics.

Dear Sir,—Bridge Foot of late has been the rendezvous of _increased political .activities. In

addition to the dissertations of Socialist scribes, we are having the diatribes of the Tariff Reformers, and the delectable deliberations of the Liberal henchmen, and as time progresses developments will perhaps become interesting. A week last Thursday we had a discourse upon the evils of Free Trade and the bounteous benefits Tariff Reform would prOide' at which meeting serious mis-statements were made. And on Monday last we had an excellent oration upon the blessings of Free Trade and the iniquities of Mineral Royalties. At both meetings statements were made upon which, I think, some comment ought to be made. At the Tariff Reform meeting a question was put to one of the speakers asking him if Railway Nationalisation would not bring in a larger revenue to the State, and gii'e a great & stimulus to trade than Tariff Reform, even admitting for the moment that Tariff Reform would do all the Tariff Reformers claim for it? To this question came the curt and entirely erroneous reply that the railways did'nt pay. I think such a statement ought not to go unchallanged and in reply I will quote from the " Daily Mail" Year book, "The value of the paid up capital of the railways of the,United Kingdom in 1905 was £1,282,801,000; the average rate of interest 3-43 per cent ; the gross receipts £113,531,010; wOrking expenses £70,064,663; net receipts £43,466,356. Tariff Reform lecturers seem to have the delinquency of not being conversant with fact—a great failing with a good many political orators. _ _

_ At Monday night's Meeting I listened to .Mr. J. Howes and I waS in entire agreement with him on the questions' of Free Trade and Mineral Royalties, but a statement he made during question time I feel compelled to contest the validity of. In reply to a question dealing with the Poor Law he assured the audience that we should always have poor people and supported the statement by quoting the Bible as say- ing" The poor ye always have with you." Whether the circumstances under which Jesus spoke these words are taken into consideration or not and the context added to the words and meaning he wished to convey I will not at present bring into the discussion. Either Mr. Howes meant to make his audience believe that for lome purpose, or he must be devoid of all the knowledge that economists place at our disposal. But we will take the statement as we lind it expressed and take it that he believes that there

will always be poor people. To understand why he believes that, without any indication of his proofs, suggests that he believes there is insufficient to go round or there is a sufficiency and it is withheld by someone who intends to withhold it. But we have ample evidence to prove that there is enough pro- duced to provide every man, woman and child in the land with a decent existence. We produce yearly- between £1,700,000,000 to £1,800,000,000 which averages about £2OO per family per year. With our chaotic, anarchistic methods we now produce enough but the produce is not properly distributed. It is esti- mated that 5 million persons take £830,000,000 and 38 million persons take £880,000,000 out of a total of £1,710,000,000. Mr. Howes is either ignorant of these facts or his statement is an assurance that the Liberal party do not intend to grapple with poverty. If his plea is ignorance he has no right to stand upon a public platform and make such nonsensical state- ments without beinc, reminded of it. If be is the medium of the Liberal party and they have no inten- tions of reducing poverty, I wish he would convey their intentions in plain terms.—l am yours, LABOUR OMNIA

===== 0002601_19090522_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB.

ANNUAL MEETING.

Some Criticisms and Changes.

There was an exceptionally large attendance at the annual meeting of the Warrington Football Club which was held in the Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom on Thursday evening. Alderman D. Tinnion (president) presided and WPS accompanied on the platform by the Committee: Messrs. T. Bowers, W. Chalmers, T. Davies, F. Barber, W. Hewitt, T. Locker, H. Ashton (hon. secretary), and T. Pemberton (hon. treasurer).

===== 0002601_19090522_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
COMMITTEE CRITICISED.

Mr. E. A. SANDERS moved and Mr. P. LAKE seconded the adoption of the Secretary's report, which was summarised in last week's "Examiner."

Mr. E. ENGLAND said that in former years he had been able to eulogise the committee, but he was sorry to say he could not do so on this cccusion. He regretted the fact because he had a great feeling of respect for the committee, most of hom bad rendered considerable service to the'club. They had not had a satisfactory seasoniby any means, especially from a playing point of view. The club had the opportunity each season. of striving for four cups—the South West Lancashire, the Lanca- shire, Northern Union, and League championships. During the past season they had not won one trophy. When they looked at their position in the League for some years past, it had been anything but creditable to a club of their standing, especially when they con- sidered the talent they had had at their disposal. I hey had no fewer than six internationals playing for them to-day and one or two of them probably had no superiors in the whole of the Northern Union. One of the few bright spots in the season was the excellent playing of the Combination team, upon which they were to be congratulated. That really reflected all the greater discredit on the members of the first team. It seemed to him that both the committee and the players niust bear the responsibility for the position the Club found itself in to-day. They bad failed to keep up with the pace of the times. They had been somewhat asleep. The race and the honours to-day went to the strenuous. The Club should provide more and better accommodation for spectators. (Applause). Then they would probably get their share of the County games and semi-finals. The accommodation could be increased without a very great outlay, and that would be more than recouped in a reasonable time. The motto of the Club was "Excelsior." Let them nevei cease striving to place their flag on the highest summit of fame and make the Club a worthy institution of the the town, so that the inhabitants as well as the members would be proud of it. (Applause). Mr. S. WRI GET said he did not intend to condemn the Committee wholesale for the result of the season's work. There had been many causes why the club had not attained the position they desired. Be believed the committee were to some extent responsible for their not having reached a higher place in the League table. If they had taken action earlier in the season in procur- ing players, instead of leaving it until the season was well advanced and they had lost a-few matches, be did not think there would have been cause for com- plaint. The club ought to aspire to League honours because interest was chiefly c.,ntred in that competition. They knew from past experience that when the club had secured a good player the sum paid for him had very soon come back into the club coffers, because they bad larger gates. He asked the Members not to single out any individual of the committee for criticism. From past experience he knew that members of the committee had unpleasant duties to perform which were apt to make them unpopular with the players and club members. Finan- cially the season had worked out very well, considering. the conditions that obtained. He blared thecommittee for the selection of the team which did duty in the first round of the cup ties. (Applause.) He did not believe anyone who had followed the games closely could have come to any other conclusion but that Lees was not a suitable man for the cup tie team. He would not say a disrespectful word of Lees, who bad done yeoman service for the Club, but his condition and his play during the past season did not warrant his selection for that match. However, they ought not to be too hard upon the committee, for they were all liable to err.

Mr: E. OSBORNE said Mr. Wright's speech had been somewhat of a whitewashing character. The committee as a whole were responsible for the work of the season and no single man should be picked out for com- mendation or denunciation. It would, he contended pay the club far better to invest some of its money in first class players rather than in municipal stock. (Applause). If they had a committee who were alive to the situation, who recognized that if the club was to be successful it must depend upon its players, they would when they had once decided to get a man go straight after him, instead of as in the case of Bolewski, when they had a very good opportunity of acquiring one of the finest men in Northern Union foot) all, allowing him to go. He understood they missed him through some unfortunate accident on the part of one of the officials of the club, whereas another club,which was not so strong financially was able to get him. That appeared to be sufficient condemnation of the committee as a whole and not of any particular member. The report stated that "to keep the players' attentive to -their duties is a very troublesome matter." If a committee was a strong committee, however, it ought to have a strong reserve of good players and if a man would not attend to his play and do his best for the club, it was for the committee to place him on the reserve and play a strong man who would make a better show. He doubted whether other players as well as Bolewski could not have bten obtained with a little more effort by the committee. They must have a strong committee and a little change would not fito any harm. If it had not been for the bar, they would have made a big loss this year. The committee ought to be enlarged, for it was not fair to expect the members to attend three nights a week during the season. They would also avoid duplication and get very much Jotter results. In the A," team they bad excellent men who worked honestly and played for all they were worth. He confessed it had been a greater pleasure to him often enough to watch the Combination team than the first team. (Applause). There was no reason why good players in the A" team should not graduate for the first team. (Applause). Mr. J. WHITHAm said the committee had no doubt done their best, but that best was not very good. ( Laughter). As to Bolewski, it might te that they had done well to let him go, for there was no guarantee that he would come back to England next season. He contended that Warrington ought to be among the first four in the League and that they ought to have reached the third or fourth round of the Northern Union Cup. (Applause). The man who put Lees on the team which met Leeds ought not to be allowed to remain on the committee. If they had selected Jordan, they would have gone to Wakefield. (Applause). If the club could not do any better than last season it could not do any worse.. (Laughter). _ _

Mr. T. DAviis said it was easy for members of the club to come down and talk about getting first class players, as if such players could be obtained as easily as plucking blackberries in Septemter. No club in the Northern Union paid as high prices for players as the Warrington club had done this season and he defied any member to mention a club which had made thesame captures. Oldham, with £4,500 in hand, had been after half backs all the season and could not get them. The could no doubt do with better accommodation on the ground, but the committee's hands had been tied because they only signepl a new lease at the beginning of the season, and it would not have looked well to spend £2OO Or £3OO in enlarging the ground when they did not know whether they would be retaining it or not. The Com- mittee thought they bad done very well not to get Bolewski.

The report was then adopted. THE FINANCES.

Mr. W. BARNES and Mr. S. WRIGHT seconded the adoption of the treasurer's report and abstract of accounts. •

Two members wished to know under what head the sums realised by the transfer of D. -shel-wood and R. Bate were included.

Mr. E. OSBORNE congratulated the treasurer on his statement and said Mr. Pemberton had done a tremendous amount of work exceedingly well. The speaker proceeded to contend that the accounts for the bar ought to le kept separate just as those for the sports and works competition were. The members should also be able to tell from the report how much the Club had made during, the year, and not have to refer to the previous year's report to ascertain the profit. Each report should be sufficient in itself. The meeting would be surprised to learn that the Club

apart from the bar, had made a loss. The CHAIRMAN: No, no. Mr. OSBORNE : I think lam right. The CHAIRMAN : You know very well, as a profes- sional auditor, that part of the charges you are reckon- ing against income would in business concerns be debited against capital instead of being paid out of revenue. Mr. OSBORNE : I think you are wrong there. Con- tinuing, he said the statement on the face of it appeared to show a profit of £lOO, whereas there was only a profit of £32, and as the bar made £75, they really lost on the year's transanctions £43. That was not shown on the face of the accounts, and it ought to be, so that they would know each year how the club was going on, without referring to previous accounts. Mr. WRIGHT asked if it was not a fact that they had lost money for years if they reckoned it on the basis laid down by Mr. Osborne? What they bad been accumulating for two or three years had accrued from the bar and the sports. Mr. OSBORNE : The club has made a profit in previous years when we had not a bar. Mr. J. E. WARREN, revert lug to the arrangement of a new base, asked who were the signatories of the original lease. The CHAIRMAN said he believed they were Messrs. JobriEvans, G. Smith, J. E. Warren and H. Most of the clubs had followed his' example. (Applause.) Mr. ENDLA.ND supported Mr. Osborne in his conten- tion that it should be made easier to arrive at the season's profit. Mr. PEMBERTON said it was easy to find the profit if they took last year's balance of assets over the liabilities and compared it with this year's. A separate account was kept for the bar and it showed that last season they took in cash £l4B 2s. 2d.; tickets for players, Ssc., £B2 4s; and ended with a stock value £32 lie. id. They began the season with a stock of £3l 13s. ; purchases and licences cost £133 16s. 4d. and barmen's wages amounted to £2l 17s. 6d., leaving a profit on the bar of £75 10s. sd. Mr. OSBORNE : That is what we ought to have printed. The accounts were then approved. • COMMITTEE ENLARGED. The next business was :the consideration of several proposals which had been handed in to increase tit number of the committee. The CHAIRMAN said the committee felt they bad more work than they could reasonably be expected to carry out and they were in favour of enlarglng the com- mittee from six to eight non-playing members. • Mr. WARREN thereupon withdiew his proposition that the committee should consist of nine non-playing membe:s. He remarked that the committee room was not big enough to accommodate any more. It, was insanitary, ill-lighted, badly furnished, and not " ear- proof." . WARREN moved : "That a professional auditor or auditors be appointed." He thought the change would only be bringing the Club into up-to- date management. They had a turnover of £4,000, so that it was a big business. Needless to say, he had every confidence in the integrity and honesty of the present auditors but he thought it advisable to have a professional auditor. Mr. T. LEAH seconded. • Mr. H. ASHTON (hon. secretary) said thee if they decided to appoint an accountant to do the, work it would mean an expesiditure of £2O or £3O a year, possibly more. The expenses of the Club were gradu- ally increasing every year. It bad been strongly advo- cated that new players should be obtained and that would mean an additional outlay' In view of the greater demands that would have to be faced next season, their policy should be one of retrenchment. The outside clubs bad perfect confidence in Mr. Pernberton,for they knew that when they came to Warrington they received every penny they were entitled to. He was not satisfied any more than the other members were with the season's results, but he did not cry over spilt milk and he intended in the future to do all be could to put the affairs of the Club into that healthy condition which every member so Areatly desired. In the whole of his experience he had never known a committee that worked harder or that was composed of 'more conscientious men than the War- aington Football Club Committee. He would not do their work for £5O a year. They bad attended at least 150 meetings in the year, and altllough they might have made mistakes they had done what they considered best in the interests of the Club. He had disagreed with them in some ways, and he ventured to think that if they had carried out some of his suggestions and those of another member some of the ill-feeling which had been shown that night would probably have been avoided. They, however, acted from honest motives, and if only the general body of the members knew some- thing of the history of the work, of their anxiety in trying to get the best players, they would not have grumble like they had done that night. WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER. It was a really brilliant opening that the favourite Manchester resort, the White City, enjoyed list Saturday and clearly demonstrated that each year the place is growing in popular estimation as a play ground for the people of all well behaved classes. For the present summer many and very great improvements have been made in the character of the many shows that are to be found in the various buildings, etc., but also in the new enterprises that are in evidence as one enters and walks about the grounds. The largest and by many experts considered the best Skating-Rink in the country has been opened, and bn Saturday more than 3,000 ladies and gentleman availed themselves of the splendid floor, while every day during the week several hundred skaters have been in evidence at each of the three daily sessions, two in the afternoon, and one at night. The very cheap prices charged for skating at the White City causes more slch.e.s to use their rink than all the others of Manchester in this warm weather. The great Fire Show," Fighting the Flames," will be given its first complete performance Saturday, May 22nd. and as this is the largest and most thrilling outdoor spectacle ever brought to England it will easily divide attention with the other attractions of the White City.

ROYAL VISIT.

The King and Queen Coining to Warrington on July 6th.

ARRANGEMENTS TO DATE.

Warrington is to have the great honour of a visit 'rom their Majesties the King and Queen on Tuesday, fuly 6th. The welcome news was contained in the 'ollowing letter, which his Worship the Mayor Alderman W. J. Forshaw) received on Monday from the Earl of Derby : Derby House, St. James's Square, S.W.

May 14th, 1909. Sir,--I have the honour to inform you that Lord Knollys has given me permission to announce that their Majesties have most graciously. consented, after the review in Worsley Park on July 6th, to return to Knowsley by motor, passing through Warrington en route. The route that it is prop sed His Majesty should follow from Worsley is the one that goes just outside Patricroft., through Higher Mara and Hollins Green.

I am, Sir, ' Your obedient Servant, DERBY.

His WORSHIP THE MAYOR OF WARRINGTON

As already announced, His Majesty is visiting Lancashire for the purpose of reviewing the East and West Lancashire Territorial Forces, and of ,opening the new Manchester Infirmary. During his stay in the 'North, his Majesty, accompanied by the Queen and Princess Victoria, will stay at Knowsley, the seat of the Earl of Derby. The inspection of the West Lancashire Force and-presentation of colours will take place at Knowsley on Monday, July sth. The 4th V.B. South Lancashire Regiment (Warrington Terri- torials) will, it is expected, be represented by 25 officers and 800 men.

On Tuesday the King and Queen, accompanied by the Earl of Derby and several other distinguished visitors, will travel from Knowsley by the London and North-Western Railway. They are expected to arrive at the London-road Station, Manchester, about 11-30 in the morning of July 6. It was originally announced that they would arrive at the Central Station, but there were practical difficulties in the way. Their Majesties will travel north in the handsome train kept for their special use by the London and North-Western Railway Company. Huyton, the station for Knowsley is on the L. and N.-W. 'System, and to get On to the Cheshire Lines Committee's system it would have been necessary to take the Royal train by a circuitous route, or to take it to West Derby or Knotty Ash, where their ' Majesties and suite might have. entrained. The new route will increase the distance their Majesties will travel through the streets of Manchester by almost half a mile. Their Majesties will be met brthe Lord Mayor of Manchester (Iklr. Alderman Edward Holt) and the membefs of the Reception Committee. The Lora Mayor will present an address from the Cor- poration, giving expression to the loyal feelings of the citizens and warmly Welcoming their _Majesties to Manchester.

After the King's reply the Lord Mayor will present the Lady Mayoress, the Recorder (Sir Joseph F. Lees, K.C,. M.P-), the Town Clerk (Sir W. H. Talbot) and Lady Talbot, and six or seven other persons repre- sentative of the public life of the city. . The King and Queen and the members of their suite will then enter carriages, and, preceded by the Lord Mayor, will drive to the Royal Infirmary. The proceedings at the railway station and the journey from the station to the Infirmary will, it is expected, occupy half an hour, so that the Royal party will be at the Infirmary at noon. The King,and Queen will walk through the buildings, and His Majesty will then formally declare them opert. The journey from the Infirmary to Worsley, where the East Lancashire Territorial Foxe Will be reviewed, will begin at one o'clock. This time the horse carriages will be set aside in favour of the swifter automobile. The route to be taken is alonc, Oxford- road, Stretford-road, Chester-road, Derbyshire-lane, Barton-road, across the Barton Bridge of the Ship Canal, thence by Liverpool-road to Worsley Hall. The Royal party will be proceeded by, the Lord Mayor and members of the Manchester Reception Committee, who will await their Majesties' arrival at the city boundary on the journey to Worsley. It may be added that all the arrangements as to escort and the review of the Territorials at Worsley are under consideration, and will be laid before the

King. The Territorial reviews at Knowsley and Worsley will be on the lines of similar functions that his Majesty has recently attended, the chief features being the march past the saluting base and the presentation of the colours. This latter will be the most impressive part of the proceedings. The defunct Volunteer forces did not carry colours, and it will therefore, be quite a new experience for old Volunteers who have joined the new forces to feel the same regard for colours of their regiment as the line battalion. The prescribed ritual for the presentation of new colours having been observed, the King will hand them over to each unit in order of precedence. As soon as the plans for the Royal visit to Knowsley and Manchester were made public, a suggestion was made that the King should be asked to honour War- rington with a visit by travelling from Knowsley to Worsley or vice versa by motor and calling here en route. The Mayor and Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., interviewed Lord Knollys at Buckingham Palace and were very favourably received, the assurance being given that the proposal should be laid before his Majesty on his return from the Continent. The result is that the King and Queen have graciously consented to make a brief visit to Warrington on their return from Worsley. •

===== 0002601_19090522_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
LATEST ARRANGEMENTS.

Lord .Derby visited • Warrington on Thursday to meet the Mayor and several of the officials of the Corporation for the purpose of making arrangements for the Royal visit. Lord Derby definitely fixed the route from Worsley to be via Ca dishead and Hollins Green, entering Warrington from Manchester-road and proceeding alc,ng Church-street, Howard-place, Buttermarket-street, and Sankey-street to the Town Hall where the presentation of an address 'will probably be made by the Mayor on behalf of the inhabitants. It is not expected that their Majesties will stay more than ten minutes at the Town Hall. Thp presentations will be very few and will include the Mayor and Mayoress. The Royal procession will then leave by the west gate and proceed along Liver- pool-road to Sankey Bridges and thence to Knowsley. It cannot yet be Stated at what time their Majesties will reach Warrington but in view of the extensive programme to be gone through at Manchester and Worsley it will be late in the afternoon. Various suggestions have been put forth with a view to making Warrington's welcome as appropriate and impressive as• possible, but nothing definite has yet been decided upon. An idea which finds favour is that the school children should assemble on the lawn in front of the Town Hall and greet the Royal visitors with the strains of the National Anthem.

The local Territorials will probably line the streets along which the Royal procession will pass.

TO-DAY'S HORSE PARADE.

Warrington's eighth annual parade of 13orses will take place to-day (Saturday) and promises to be more successful than any of its predecessors. Several changes have been made, the most important being the abol.tion of classes, and every man whom the judges consider worthy of a prize will receive one. Arrange- ments have been made for the judging to take place in front of the Town Hall, by permission of the Mayor. Competitors will assemble as follows at 15 noon and approach the Town Hall gates (eastern side) :—Light horse turnouts, Springfield-street; Greenall, Whitley and Co's entries, Bold-street from Wesleyan Church to top of street ; Warrington Slate Co., Bold-street opposite Queen's Gardens ; Warrington Corporation, Bold-street. from Palmyra-square to Wilson Patten- street ; Peter Walker and Sons, Legh-street ; heavy miscellaneous, North Palmyra-square; reserve, Egypt and C tiro-streets. Non-competitors will form up in Parker-street. The -Mayor and Mayoress will inspec the. procession as it passes in front of the Town Hallt when the diploma's will be presented to winners as, they arrive in order. The competitors will leave by the Town Hall gates (western side) about 1.30 and pro- ceed along Sankey-street, Crosfield-street (where the procession will be re-formed), Froghall-lane, Tanners'- lane, Winwick-street, Horsemarket-street, Bridge-st., Mersey-street, Buttermarket-street, Sankey-street, and disperse on reaching IMarket Gate. The Borough Band and the Pioneer Band will play selections and there will be a collection en route on behalf of local charities.

EMPIRE DAY.

Empire Day will be celebrated on Monday and the occasion will be a memorable one for the school children of Warrington. The scholars of the senior departments of all the local day schools will march in procession to the Park, where they• are due to assemble not later than 11 a.m. It was originally intended that the gathering should take place on the lawn in front of the Town Hall, but with a view to preserving the greensward so that it may look at its best for the forthcoming Royal visit, it is expected that the children will be massed on the. large recrea-

tion ground on the Kendrick-street side orthe Park. Under the conductorship of Mr. T. D. Amos (head- master of the Hamilton-street school), they will join in singing "Now pray we for our country. Messrs. Walker's Brewery Band will accompany. His Worship the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw) will then pre- sent a medal to the best boy and a brooch to the best girl from each school. The scholars will be addressed _

v the Mayor and 'the Chairmen of the Education Committee (Councillor J. Fulton-Smith). At the close "God save the King" will be sung and the children will march back to their respective schools. Yesterday afternoon a rehearsal of the singing took place on the Recreation Ground and although it is by no means easy to train about _9,000 children to sing in time and tune, everything passed off satisfactorily.

CLOSING PUBLIC HOUSES.

APPORTIONING THE COMPENSATION AT WARRINGTON.

The Warrington Compensation Authority held a meet- ing at the Borough Police Court yesterday morning to cpnsider the renewal or refusal of the following licences which had been refered to them :—Rose and Crown, Bridge-street, alehouse, licensee, Thomas Hockenhull ; Bird-in-Hand, Bank-street, beerhouse, (ante 1859), licensee, Patrick William Roddy. The magistrates on the Bench were :—The Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Evans, Messrs. John Fairclough, R. T. Fairclough, J. W. Hayward, C. S. Burgess, H. Roberts. C. Jackson Holmes, W. Furness, H. Thornton, G. Pilling, T. Locker and A. J. Jolley. Mr. BROWNE, on behalf of the owners (Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co.) and tenants offered nia objection to the licences being placed on the compensa- tion list. With regard to the Rose and Crown, the claim was based on the lines laid down in the case of the Crown v. Cobham Brewery. The average number of barrells sold during the past three years was 277 per annum which at 14s. 6d. per barrel,—the prtfit allcrwed to the brewers in the Crown .v. Cobham Brewery case —worked out at £2OO 16s. 6d. lathe same time there had been supplied 1,389 bottles per annum, and the profit, on the basis of 7-Id, per dozen, amounted to £4l 19s. 2d. The total for beer and stout was £242 15s. Bd., and capitalized at ten years' purchase ' the amount was £2,427 16s. Bd. The average consumption of wine and spirits was 345 gallons which, at 2s. 6d. per gallon profit, realized £43 2s. 6d. That, at ten years' purchase, came to £l3l ss. The, depreciation of trade fixtures was estimated at £lll ss. (owners' £6l 55., tenant's £5O), making a total of £2,970 6s. Bd. The owners did not propose to make any claim for the value of the property, because they were advised that the rents received after a!terations would bring in an amount equal to the present assessment in schedule a. They therefore made no claim for the cost of conversion, and the total amount of their claim in respect of the Rose and Crown was £2,970 6s. Bd. The tenant had agreed, subject to the Bench's confirmation, to accept £lOO and the sum allowed for depreciation of fixtures, making a total of £l5O. The Rose and Crown was thought to be a poor house, but that view had turned out to be absolutely incorrect.

With regard to the Bird-in-Hand, an ante 1869 beer- house, Mr. Browne said the average barrellage for the last three years was 218 per annum which at 14s. 6d.per barrel profit worked out at £l5B is. The bottled beers supplied averaged 116 dozen per annum which at 7:1-d. per dozen gave £3 10s. id., making a total of £l6l lie. Id. Putting it at eleven years purchase—which was allowed by the Chester Compensation Authority for ante 1869 beerhouses—the amount was £1,777 Is. 11d. The rent received by the owners was £lB which capitalized at 18 years purchase came to £324. The depreciation of fixtures was estimated at £4O 14s.(owners' £33, tenants' £7 145.) To convert the place into a private dwelling-house would cost £2O 10s., making a total of £2,162 ss. lid. The firm gave credit for the value of the premises without a licence. The houses in Bank-street would let for nothing but common lodging- houses, and one which stood opposite the Bird-in-Hand had been empty for eighteen months. The value of the premises without the licence could not be more than £2OO.

Mr. Tliontcros.—Trade is bad. There are many empty houses. Mr. BROWNE said that deducting the £2OO left £1,962 ss. lid. The tenant had agreed, subject to the Bench's confirmation, to accept £75 in addition to the £7 14s. for depreciation. _ .

After the magistrates had deliberated in private the Mayor said the licences would be refused and the ques- tion of compensation referred to the Inland Revenue, authorities for settlement.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Beckett—Lowe.

A. wedding of considerable local interest took place at Our .Lady's Church, Latchford, on Thursday, when Mr. Allred Beckett, of Vancouver, 8.C., son of Mr. George Beckett, of Ruabon, was married to Miss Mary Agnes Lowe, daughter of Mr. Robert Lowe, auctioneer, Latchford Lodge. The Rev. Father Marrs officiated. Mr. John Eckersley was at the organ and played the" Bridal March from " Lohengrin " and other suitable selections. The. bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in corn flower crepe de chine. She was attended by Miss Gwyn Bamber, who wore a mole coloured Empire gown. Mr. Austin Lowe discharged the duties of best man. After the ceremony a reception was held at Latchford Lodge, and later the happy couple left for London. They, will set sail from Liverpool for Vancouver by the" Virginian" on Friday next. The bride's travelling dress was a -Reseda green costume. A very pretty wedding took place at the Particular Baptist Church, Legh-street, on Thursday morning, when Miss Elizabeth Anne Barton, daughter of Mrs. E. Barton, West-street, was married to Mr. John Alfred Caldwell, Padgate-lane, son of the late Mr. John Caldwell. Mr. Alexander Mackenzie officiated and Mr. J. L. Bate who was at the organ played selections by T. Mee Pattison. The bride who was given away by her brother William, was attired in a cream satin voile Empire dress trimmed with lace and a satin sash. She carried a bouquet of lilies of the valley and roses. The bridesmaids were Misses Edith Caldwell and Maggie Barton, who wore embroidered cream silk dresses. The duties of best man were performed by Mr. James Byfield. The reception was held at the bridegroom's house, Padgate-lane. . The honeymoon is to be spent in. London. Numerous presents were received and it may be added that the happy couple are going to the United States of America where they will take up their abode in a few weeks time.
